En l’emportant samedi soir (2-0), les Verts de Ian Cathro ont prolongé une série d’invincibilité historique.

En effet, l’ASSE recevait Laval pour la 22e fois à Geoffroy Guichard et le bilan est désormais de 17 victoires et 5 matchs nuls.

C’est la plus longue et plus belle série d’invincibilité des Verts dans le Chaudron contre un club de L1, L2 ou L3.

En Ligue 1, chacun des 18 clubs est déjà venu l’emporter au moins une fois à GG. En Ligue 2 en revanche, Annecy (6 matchs) et Dunkerque (5 matchs) n’ont, comme Laval, jamais connu la victoire à Sainté.

En Ligue 3 parmi les clubs déjà venus dans le Chaudron, Concarneau (1 match), La Roche sur Yon (1 match), Bourg-Péronnas (1 match), QRM (2 matchs) et Le Puy (5 matchs) complètent cette liste.

Following site-wide discussion on AI use, Nexus Mods add a "daily limit" to the spots in the new mods tab one person can hog via multiple rapid-fire uploads

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It's a "stopgap solution" while the site's staff are busy "working on more sophisticated tools"

The folks behind Nexus Mods have rolled out a new "daily limit" system which ensures modders who upload multiple works in bulk during a single 24 hour period can't take up more than one spot in the tabs dedicated to showing off new mods for different games. Instead, those who chuck up their mods in bulk will see one of their works given a spot, while the rest are neatly filed away behind it, ready to spring out if you click a bit of text indicating that modder's got other fresh works to show off.

"Nexus Mods owner and CEO Victor 'Foledinho' Folmann hinted measures related to making the glut of new mods currently uploaded on a daily basis less of a discovery nightmare in last week's announcement post about the site's current policy regarding the use of genAI in mod making. "We’ve received feedback regarding authors uploading numerous mods at once and overwhelming the Recently Added section, pushing everyone else down the page," he wrote. "This is less of an issue related to AI, but it’s becoming much more prevalent as mod production is speeding up. Thus, we’re trying out limits on the number of mods from one author that can be shown in this section to give others a better chance of getting noticed. We’re also putting tighter restrictions on uploads from newly created accounts. The first of these updates will be available soon."

Cue an an announcement on Nexus Mods' Discord server yesterday by community manager Phoenix introducing the new "daily limit", which Nexus were keen to emphasise is "an experiment" they're open to feedback on. "We know that time in the New Mods view is important for a new mod to be discovered and we believe every mod author should have equal chances of having their work seen," they wrote. "While we're working on more sophisticated tools to give you control over the mods displayed to you, we are testing a stopgap solution that we hope will address the issue for now: For the time being, the New Mods view will display one mod per mod author. This limit refreshes every twenty-four hours – so if you publish one mod every day, you won't be affected."

The All Mods, Updated, Trending, Popular, and Surprise tabs for games are unaffected by the change, so will still dedicate a tab to each work regardless of who uploaded it when. If you take a glance at the New Mods pages for the likes of Skyrim, Fallout 4, and Cyberpunk 2077 this morning, you can already see the new mod stacking in action. The most mods from the same author I've seen stacked atop each other so far is 14 Fallout 4 works piled up.

It's worth noting you can also toggle the show only one new mod per modder rule off in the new mods tab, bringing back the older layout with one slot per mod regardless of uploader.

Personally, I tend to sort by mods with the most endorsements or downloads within the past 24 hours or seven days when I'm scanning for new mods, rather than just scrolling back through the new mods tab. Though I appreciate that could mean I've been missing out on some stellar works by lesser known modders whose creations never get the traction they merit thanks to being buried under an avalanche of other works. If so, hopefully this update does help the lone mods avoid being run over by mass upload freight trains.

The 3 summer targets Blackburn Rovers should pursue again in January, including Balazs Toth replacement

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Blackburn Rovers have exceeded the expectations of many in the early stages of the 2026/27 campaign. Tony Mowbray‘s side sit 13th in the Championship after 15 games, when many expected them to be in the thick of a relegation battle.

That fate could yet await with a lot of the season left to play, but spirits are fairly high at Ewood Park. There is no hiding that the Rovers squad needs work though, with only three new summer signings failing to suitably reinforce the ranks.

New targets will be on the radar, but Blackburn Rovers identified some smart options in the summer who could be available this winter. Here, we put forward three summer targets who should be back on the radar ahead of January…

Balazs Toth has continued to resist Blackburn Rovers’ new contract efforts. It remains to be seen exactly when he leaves, but a departure seemingly awaits at some point in 2027, and the club could do with sourcing a replacement sooner rather than later.

Alex Palmer was linked with a move to Ewood Park in the last transfer window, and he could be a fine option to consider to replace Toth. The 30-year-old is out of favour at Ipswich Town under Gary O’Neil, but he is a proven Championship goalkeeper who could quickly become a dependable No. 1 for Mowbray.

Palmer is under contract until 2028 at Portman Road but given that he has not made a Premier League matchday squad this season, the Tractor Boys should not have an issue letting him go for a fairly cheap fee.

Blackburn Rovers have one of Europe’s most clinical strikers in Andri Gudjohnsen at the moment, but competition beyond him is limited. Yuki Ohashi is the only other out-and-out centre-forward, so more depth would not go amiss.

Daniel Jebbison was targeted by Rovers in the summer and a loan-to-buy deal was even agreed, but he ended up staying at Bournemouth. Given the fact that he has played only four times so far this season, the 23-year-old would benefit from a winter move elsewhere to find more game time.

The Canadian striker has Championship experience and has strong physical traits like Gudjohnsen. Jebbison is a strong finisher on both feet and Mowbray’s style of play and ability to develop players could help him hit prolific form.

Last but not least is a much-needed midfield reinforcement. Mowbray has expressed a desire to sign a new central midfielder in the winter, and someone who offers experience but has more years ahead of Sam Morsy and Adam Forshaw would be ideal.

Alan Browne fits this profile perfectly. The 31-year-old has played over 400 Championship games but showed on loan at Middlesbrough last season that he still has plenty in the tank, offering a well-rounded skillset, versatility and energy in the middle of the park.

Browne is way down the pecking order at Sunderland and out of contract next summer. It should be an easy deal for Blackburn Rovers to strike and he would instantly improve their options in midfield.

Photographer Killed While Capturing Vintage Airplane

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Photographer Killed While Capturing Vintage Airplane

A photographer has died after she was struck by a vintage airplane that she was taking pictures of.

Pamela Haselton, 67, was attempting to move a tripod out of the plane’s path on Friday shortly after it had landed at a private airfield in Essex County, New York.

The 1942 Aeronca L-3 single-engine, two-seat aircraft had just landed and was taxiing toward its hangar. The aircraft was being flown by James Catalano, 77, who says he did not see the camera or Haselton before the accident.

Times Union reports that both Haselton and Catalano were from Wilmington, a small town located within Adirondack Park.

Police say that Haselton had been using the tripod to take photos of the Aeronca L-3, which she had positioned on the runway. When the plane touched down and approached, she tried to move the tripod.

An autopsy found that Haselton suffered massive injuries, and despite attempts to save her life at the scene, she was pronounced dead. Her death has been ruled an accident.

Tributes have been paid to Haselton, who, according to her friends, lived life to the full.

“Pam had a deep love for nature and the beautiful Adirondack region she called home. An avid hiker and passionate photographer, she was often out the door in pursuit of a beautiful sunrise, a breathtaking sunset, or another opportunity to capture the natural wonders around her,” the Town of Wilmington writes on Facebook.

“Whether exploring woodland trails, photographing mountain landscapes, or admiring the changing seasons, Pam found endless joy in the beauty of the world around her. Through her photography, she shared that beauty with countless others, inspiring an appreciation for the places she held so dear.”

According to Times Union, the Wilmington E. M. Cooper Memorial Public Library held an exhibition of her photos back in May. It also paid tribute to her.

“Pam was a treasured member of our Library Friends and a former board member. She was an absolute dynamo, with an infectious spirit and an energy that touched so many in our community.”

Hasleton’s friend Wendy Grossman tells Times Union that the photographer was the type to “climb a mountain at 3 AM to see the sunrise.”

“The whole community is kind of in mourning,” adds Grossman. “We’re grieving.”

Bundesliga and NFL announce extension of global collaboration

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The Bundesliga and the NFL (National Football League) have announced the extension of their global collaboration via their Memorandum of Understanding. The collaboration will see the continuation of cross-sport initiatives, whilst producing new fan experiences worldwide.

The Bundesliga and NFL first partnered in 2022 in an attempt to grow the relationship between American and European football.

During this time, there have been various initiatives throughout the US, including a series of physical and digital events. This includes former Bayern Munich star Rafinha joining former New England Patriots players Brian Hoyer and Sebastian Vollmer for a watch party ahead of the Patriots’ home game against the Buffalo Bills in December 2025, while Borussia Dortmund legend Patrick Owomoyela announced the Pittsburgh Steelers’ sixth-round selection at the 2026 NFL Draft.

Both organisations will continue to collaborate in several key areas, such as innovation, digital and experiential marketing practices and opportunities for cross-sport content. Most notably for fans on the ground, they can expect to see future collaborations between stars from both leagues at major events.

Over the past four years, the NFL has also played five regular-season games in three Bundesliga stadiums across Germany in Munich (FC Bayern München), Frankfurt (Eintracht Frankfurt), and Berlin (Hertha Berlin). This could continue in future seasons; however, Bundesliga matches in the US are not a topic of discussion.

The Bundesliga has also been present at the last two NFL Super Bowls, with Bundesliga legends Claudio Pizzaro and Roman Weidenfeller representing the league in New Orleans and San Francisco.

Upon the announcement, NFL Commissioner Roger Goodell said: “The partnership between the NFL and the Bundesliga demonstrates the value of bringing two of the world’s leading sports leagues together. By extending our collaboration, we can continue to learn from each other, create new experiences for fans and explore innovative ways to grow our sports in Germany, the United States and around the world.”

designboom listens: objects of common interest embraces the beauty of unexpected contrasts

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

from new order and talking heads to aphex twin and david bowie, the athens- and new york-based design studio shares the songs that shape its creative mood, evoke memories, and offer unexpected parallels with the way it approaches design.

For Objects of Common Interest, design and music share an interest in bringing different elements together to create something unexpected. The Athens- and New York-based studio, founded by Eleni Petaloti and Leonidas Trampoukis, works across objects, interiors, and spatial installations, exploring how form, color, material, and perception can shape the way we experience our surroundings. Their playlist moves between emotional melodies, hypnotic repetition, and experimental sounds, echoing the different qualities they explore through design.

This relationship also comes through in the latest public installation by the studio, titled Out of This World – A Field of Conditions, presented in the Miami Design District. Bringing together six different conditions that alter how people perceive and experience their surroundings, the series of interactive sculptures explores the possibility of seeing familiar environments in new ways. Music offers a similar point of departure in the duo’s playlist, which moves between New Order, Talking Heads, Chet Baker, Gorillaz, Aphex Twin, and David Bowie. Some selections bring back particular atmospheres or memories, while others help shift perspective, break creative blocks, or focus attention on the smallest details. Read on for the duo’s answers and discover the tracks that accompany their practice.

What song best captures your creative practice? Objects of Common Interest (OoCI): Bizarre Love Triangle – New Order. We like the coexistence of emotion, structure, repetition, and unexpected energy. Different elements come together in a way that feels both precise and unpredictable. There is something about that tension that relates to how we approach design.

What is the very first track you play to clear the air and get inspired when starting a new project? OoCI: This Must Be the Place – Talking Heads. It has an effortless optimism that we love. It’s familiar but somehow always feels fresh. A good reminder that inspiration doesn’t necessarily come from looking for something extraordinary, but from seeing ordinary things differently.

Is there a song that instantly takes you back to a specific memory or place from your past? OoCI: Let’s Get Lost – Chet Baker. There’s something incredibly nostalgic about Chet Baker’s voice. It takes us back to a certain atmosphere rather than a specific moment, a feeling of being somewhere between the past and the present.

What song is stuck in your head right now? OoCI: Coming Up Roses – Harry Styles. Sometimes a song just stays with you, without needing a particular reason.

What is your ultimate 2 AM studio track, the one you secretly know every word to? OoCI: On Melancholy Hill – Gorillaz. It has this beautiful balance of melancholy and optimism. Dreamy, slightly strange, and incredibly addictive. Perfect for those late hours when you’re tired but don’t really want to stop working.

What song do you put on when you are stuck and need a breakthrough? OoCI: Love to Angie – Red Astaire. Sometimes the best way to move forward is to stop overthinking. Music can shift your energy completely, and suddenly things start making sense again.

What track is dominating your speakers this week, and is it influencing what you are making right now? OoCI: What Else Is There? – Röyksopp. We love its slightly mysterious atmosphere and the way it builds tension. We wouldn’t say music directly influences the forms we create, but it definitely influences the mood and energy around the process.

What song helps you slow down and focus on the tiny, quiet details? OoCI: Avril 14th – Aphex Twin. It’s incredibly simple and delicate. Every note feels intentional, and the space between the notes is just as important. It reminds us how much can happen through very little.

If people would wear headphones while experiencing your latest work, what one song should be playing? OoCI: Tainted Love – Soft Cell. There’s something wonderfully theatrical and slightly eccentric about it. It has a strong personality, a certain strangeness, and an irresistible rhythm. We like the idea of experiencing our work with that kind of unexpected soundtrack.

What is your favorite album cover art? OoCI: Power, Corruption & Lies – New Order. We love the unexpected relationship between a classical painting of flowers and a coded graphic system. It brings together something deeply romantic and something almost scientific. Two seemingly unrelated languages coexist and create an entirely new identity.

What is a track that best describes your favorite project you have worked on so far? OoCI: Sound and Vision – David Bowie. For Out of This World – A Field of Conditions, our latest public installation in the Miami Design District. The project brings together six different conditions that alter how we perceive and experience our surroundings. We like how the song suggests a world where sound, color, and imagination coexist. It captures that sense of curiosity and the possibility of experiencing something familiar in an entirely unexpected way.

NASA Scientists Show How Gravitational Waves Can Reveal New Worlds

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

The LISA (Laser Interferometer Space Antenna) observatory, now in development, will detect gravitational waves, the tiny ripples in space-time caused by orbiting masses. Now two studies led by NASA astrophysicists show in new detail how LISA could reveal a new and unique population of exoplanets — worlds beyond our solar system — throughout our galaxy.

“The most common source for LISA is expected to be double white dwarf systems, which are binaries composed of white dwarfs in tight orbits,” said Casey McGrath, a postdoctoral researcher at the University of Maryland, Baltimore County and NASA’s Goddard Space Flight Center in Greenbelt, Maryland. “If an exoplanet also orbits within one of these systems, its presence becomes imprinted on the gravitational wave signal in a way we’ll be able to uncover.”

Two papers published Tuesday in The Astrophysical Journal detail the findings. The first, authored solely by McGrath, examines the detection method and determines the planet masses and orbital periods where it would work best. The second paper, by McGrath and coauthors, investigates how such planets could be confirmed using NASA’s James Webb Space Telescope.

The possibility of detecting exoplanets around LISA double white dwarf systems was first proposed by Japanese astronomer Naoki Seto in 2008.

Led by ESA (European Space Agency), the LISA mission is slated for launch in the mid-2030s. As a collaborative partner, NASA is contributing the telescopes, other critical hardware, and engineering and scientific support as part of its mission to better understand how the universe works.

The LISA detection method is similar to one called radial velocity that uses light and is already employed to find exoplanets around nearby stars. As a planet orbits, its gravity tugs on its host star, imparting a small reflex motion, or wobble, onto it. This wobble causes the star’s spectrum to oscillate, with spectral lines shifting from redder wavelengths as the star moves away from us to bluer ones as the star moves toward us. The shift is due to a phenomenon called the Doppler effect.

“Something similar occurs with gravitational waves,” said Scott Noble, a theoretical astrophysicist at NASA Goddard and a coauthor on the confirmation paper. “By themselves, double white dwarfs will emit gravitational waves at a nearly continuous frequency. Add a sufficiently massive planet to the system, and its presence causes the binary to experience reflex motion, adding a Doppler modulation to the gravitational signal.”

For LISA, this modulation will appear as sidebands, secondary signals at both higher and lower frequencies than the primary one.

McGrath likens the situation to modern radio communication, where some of the energy of a main radio carrier frequency goes into forming sidebands at adjacent upper and lower frequencies that carry messages (like voices or music). For gravitational waves, the sidebands carry messages about the presence of a planet.

White dwarfs are the Earth-sized remnants of stars like the Sun. While exoplanets have been detected around a few isolated white dwarfs, none have yet been found around a binary white dwarf, which means the LISA detections would represent a distinct new exoplanet population.

Astronomers estimate that our Milky Way galaxy contains 10 million double white dwarf systems with orbital periods of less than an hour, which generate gravitational waves in the right range for LISA to find. They expect LISA will resolve about 10,000 individual systems, as well as around 100 mixed binaries — with white dwarfs, neutron stars, or black holes — orbiting in similar periods.

“Once LISA captures its first exoplanet detections, it will be critical for us to validate some of them,” said Mary Anne Limbach, an assistant research scientist at the University of Michigan and a co-author on the confirmation paper. “These joint detections will allow us to establish that the gravitational signals correspond to genuine planets rather than false positives.”

The team described a new method for confirming these planets by taking advantage of the Webb telescope’s infrared sensitivity. A planet in a double white dwarf system produces a larger portion of infrared light than what’s expected from the stars themselves. Webb’s instruments can easily detect this infrared excess out to a distance of at least 500 light-years.

Using this technique for a test case system at this distance, the researchers find that Webb could detect an exoplanet about five times Jupiter’s mass, while LISA could detect a sub-Jupiter exoplanet under ideal circumstances.

The LISA observatory will consist of a trio of spacecraft following Earth as it orbits the Sun. They will form a vast triangular array stretching 1.6 million miles (2.5 million kilometers) on each side. Each spacecraft will include two telescopes that will use infrared laser beams to simultaneously transmit and receive signals between adjacent spacecraft. Through these telescopes, the spacecraft will measure miniscule changes — smaller than the width of a helium atom — in their relative distances, the signals of passing gravitational waves.

Gravitational waves were predicted by Albert Einstein’s 1916 general theory of relativity and first detected by ground-based observatories in 2015. The waves form whenever massive objects accelerate, such as two stars in orbit around each other. They travel through space-time, moving at the speed of light, and are unaffected by objects they encounter along the way. These properties make them a valuable tool for probing the cosmos.

Additional NASA contributions to the mission include the laser system, devices to manage the buildup of electric charge on the proof masses, data analysis for identifying and characterizing individual gravitational wave sources, and additional scientific and engineering expertise.

To learn more about the LISA mission, visit:

NASA explores the unknown in air and space, innovates for the benefit of humanity, and inspires the world through discovery.

'The first species we might be able to communicate with, interact with vocally, might be a bird': An animal researcher explains how she decoded birdsong

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Julie Elie, an ethologist at the University of California, Berkeley, tells Live Science's Kenna Hughes-Castleberry about using AI to decode the vocalizations of zebra finches.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Bird calls are far more than pleasant sounds in nature. They are complex forms of communication that can reveal how birds think, learn, cooperate and respond to their surroundings. From intricate songs used to attract mates to alarm calls that warn others of danger, vocalizations offer valuable clues about avian intelligence and social organization.

Julie Elie is an animal behavior researcher and associate project scientist at the University of California, Berkeley. She recently won the 2026 Coller Dolittle Challenge for Two-Way Interspecies Communication for her work in studying and deciphering zebra finch communication.

For more than a decade, Elie observed the social behavior of birds, recording thousands of sounds and using machine learning combined with behavioral experiments to decode specific calls.

In this interview, Elie discusses how zebra finches learn and use vocalizations, what their calls reveal about intelligence and social relationships and how artificial intelligence (AI) contributed to decoding birdsong.

Dr Julie Elie is a computational neuroethologist who studies how social animals communicate. Her research combines behavioral experiments, neuroscience, and computational tools to reveal how animals produce communication sounds, what information they transmit in their vocalizations and how they perceive the meaning of vocalizations.

Kenna Hughes-Castleberry: What made you study birds and birdsong?

Julie Elie: The beginning of my work with birds dates from my Ph.D. I've always been super interested in animal behavior in general. But I think as a young person, I was more attracted by animals that we relate more easily to, which are mammals, right? Dogs, cats, horses.

I wanted to do a Ph.D. on animal behavior and, more specifically, on relationships between animals. I was interested in how animals interact with each other and what kind of social organization they establish.

[Someone suggested I] work on the zebra finch, and I said, "OK, I've never taken the time to really observe birds very well; might as well start now."

I realized that it's difficult to observe birds because they're not close to you. They can fly wherever they want. When you are out and about in town, in the countryside, and you see birds, they're here, and then they're gone. You don't get to see a lot of their vocal interactions or a lot of their social interactions.

So here I was, studying the zebra finch in cages where they were fully interacting with each other. And the question was, what kind of social organization do they have?

I realized quickly that they were not doing a dominance hierarchy [which was previously thought to be the case], but instead, the most important thing in their life was their partner. They were doing pair bonds, and that's the core of their social organization, [with] males and females pairing for life.

Studying birdsong can help researchers like Elie better understand how the birds navigate their complex environments.

KHC: Why is it important to study birdsong, and what can it teach us about intelligence and behavior?

JE: A level of complexity we were trying to understand was, what are the neural mechanisms that enable those birds to do this process of listening to something and copying it, and reproducing it, and doing a comparison between what they're producing and what they're hearing?

The zebra finch is a just-right middle ground level of cognitive ability that is interesting, where you see this pattern of copying vocalization and producing it. But you can look through the avian kingdom, and some species show more complex behaviors.

We know that there are some species that are able to not only imitate their own vocalizations from their own species, their dad, but they're able to copy other species. So, here in California, for instance, a mockingbird is a bird that is really known to be able to reproduce a bunch of different sounds and is actually incorporating this in their vocalizations.

Parrots are also well known for that because we have them as pets, and they're able to reproduce our own vocalizations as humans. This is kind of crazy, when you think about it, because they don't have the same vocal tract as we do. It's smaller, and they have a different system. I think a high cognitive level is when an animal is able to reproduce a sound that is not in their "native language," let's say, and use it for a goal. We do see this in parrots ‪—‬ they're able to ask you for something or even ask the dog to shut up sometimes, which is interesting. They're able to really use those vocalizations, such as "This is not mine, but I communicate with another species this way."

It shows you how smart they can be, and by studying vocal communication in animals, we are getting a window of what cognitive abilities they have and how the usage reveals their level of smartness.

KHC: Could you walk me through how you ended up decoding bird vocalizations?

JE: I spent countless hours observing zebra finches, and I realized that I could progressively do a match between the social context and the acoustic characteristics of the sound produced. I could recognize these sounds and understand what they "mean."

It's like at home with your cat or dog: Within a couple of months, you figure out every time the cat is doing this kind of sound —‬ "meow" — ‬it means he wants to get out or he's hungry, or he wants to cuddle. Same thing with the dog. It's the same process, but instead of doing this with cats or dogs, I did this with birds and constituted the first acoustic dictionary of zebra finch calls.

However, how do you know you are correct? That you correctly identified the sounds that correspond to the "words" in that species?

Pet owners can often differentiate between different vocalizations in their pets, such as different meowing noises for their cat.

I thought that the best confirmation of whether I was right or not could only come from the zebra finches themselves. Up to now, nobody had found a way to systematically go through the vocal repertoire of an animal and confirm if each identified call type was indeed perceived as a word in that species' language.

To achieve this somewhat impossible discussion with a bird, we designed an operant conditioning experiment where the bird is asked to classify vocalizations that were previously recorded from other zebra finches. The game is simple and relies on two rules. The first one is that every day, only the recordings corresponding to one call type are rewarded by seeds, while the recordings from the other call types in the repertoire are not rewarded. The second rule is that the bird can interrupt recordings and skip to the next one whenever they want, like how you skip through songs on your favorite music app.

The bird is placed in a cage that has a button or a little infrared sensor that it can activate with its beak. Each time it does so, the recording of a vocalization randomly drawn from the library of call types is played. Four out of five times, birds trigger the playback of a call type that will not give them access to seeds. Because they are slightly food deprived, they only really want to listen to the rewarded playback, so they interrupt the nonrewarded calls and only listen for the calls that are going to be rewarded.

Every day, the bird has to find out which call type or "word" is rewarded. They usually figure it out pretty rapidly and then start interrupting the playback of all the other call types. Using this task, we could test a different call type every dayIf they had not been able to do it, it would tell us that the call type we had identified was in fact not existing in their language. If they were able to do it ‪—‬ and spoiler alert, they were! ‪—‬ it meant that we had correctly identified that call type.

The birds told us yes, basically —‬ the organization that you're proposing is correct.

But then we made a surprising discovery. We noticed that birds were making some rare and systematic mistakes. We wondered if these mistakes were coming from the acoustic similarity of the call types — how you might mistake, for instance, the word "glove" for "love" or "hurt" for "heart." Or if they were coming from the semantic similarity of the call types — you could maybe mistake "love" for "heart" if you are remembering about the general meaning of the rewarded word today, which is "love.".

In that last case, it would mean that birds truly have an understanding of their call types' meaning. We found out that birds' errors could be better explained by the meaning [the semantic similarity] of the call types. That is a big discovery in the field of animal communication.

I'm thinking that the first species we might be able to communicate with, interact with vocally, might be a bird.

KHC: Do you think we are headed toward actually being able to communicate with birds, or do you think it's more that we're still testing the waters and we still need to learn the rest of the language before we get there?

JE: When we say "talking with birds," I think we need to understand that it's not going to be a language. Right now, I am studying the zebra finch; it doesn't mean that I would be able to communicate with a crow once I have a repertoire. So there's this component of when we talk about birds, there are many different species.

I think there's more traction in this particular part of the animal kingdom than in other parts, just because birds are really talkative. Birds use a lot of their vocalizations to communicate with each other. So because of that, I'm thinking that the first species we might be able to communicate with, interact with vocally, might be a bird, just because they are very talkative.

For now, I found all those words. But we need to understand how those words are [strung] together. So it's not necessarily the case that we need to understand the rules of the dynamic exchanges between individuals or how fast you're responding, but how you are responding and whether that makes sense or not.

They [also] have [body] postures. They have specific displays at the same time they're producing the vocalizations. And if we really want to achieve full communication with another species, we need to get to that level.

It's not tomorrow for sure, it's probably not next year, but I think in the range of 10 years we should get there. Especially with how fast the domains of AI and robotics are. I have some hope that we should be able to get to that point.

KHC: Is there one takeaway you would want our readers to know about your work?

JE: Just one. The most important thing for readers that are not specifically scientists is to realize that, by studying animal behavior more generally, researchers are discovering the intelligence of animals. This is opening our eyes to other species and realizing that we're not the only species that is intelligent on this planet.

This is a lesson to us as another species on this planet, that we are not the only ones that are smart and we need to take this into account in how we are evolving.

We need to realize how we're affecting them and realize that they also have their own internal lives, and maybe we should start to respect them a little bit more.

Editor's note: Some of this interview was conducted via email as part of a follow-up conversation. The interview has been edited lightly for clarity and length.

"No NASA mission has ever done this before."

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Twin NASA spacecraft are wrapping up a year-long escapade, ready to make a beeline to the Red Planet.

The Escape and Plasma Acceleration and Dynamics Explorers (ESCAPADE) spacecraft launched in November 2025 on the second-ever flight of Blue Origin's New Glenn rocket. But rather than head straight to Mars, the two spacecraft dawdled through a looping trajectory. Now, they are back in Earth's neighborhood where they tackled a crucial engine burn unusually close to Earth that — if all goes well — will set them on track for Mars.

"No NASA mission has ever done this before," said Rob Lillis, a planetary and space physicist at the University of California, Berkeley and principal investigator of the ESCAPADE mission. "For most missions, it just isn't necessary."

That's because traditionally NASA's Mars missions have been carefully choreographed within cosmic launch constraints, leaving Earth during a brief window of planetary alignment that only occurs every 26 months. ESCAPADE was meant to launch in October 2024, when Earth and Mars were favorably aligned, but delays with the New Glenn rocket forced the mission to sit out the precious launch window.

Rather than wait to launch this fall, NASA decided to try something new. ESCAPADE mission planners designed a "loitering" orbit, shaped like a kidney bean. It called for the twin probes to loop through that trajectory to be back at Earth in early November 2026, ready to continue on to Mars.

Once they arrived, however, the twin probes faced a major hurdle: Each had to perfectly execute an engine burn within Earth's gravity well. The maneuver is similar to the slingshot flybys that spacecraft regularly conduct to adjust their trajectories — but here, the probes had to fire their engines to amp up the effect of the burn rather than acting alone.

The first spacecraft tackled the feat on Nov. 7; the second did so on Monday (Nov. 9). And according to NASA, the burns went perfectly. "This is the first time NASA has used a powered Earth flyby to propel a spacecraft toward Mars, and we've now executed this maneuver twice, flawlessly, in just two days," Asal Naseri, acting associate flight director for NASA's Heliophysics Division said in a statement.

After just a few minor course corrections, ESCAPADE will be sailing toward a September 2027 Mars arrival.

The twin ESCAPADE spacecraft have made the most of their extended tour. Each spacecraft is about the size of two refrigerators and equipped with three scientific instruments and a couple cameras. All are tailored for studying the strange magnetic field of Mars, which is created both globally by charged particles from the sun interacting with the atmosphere and locally by areas of highly magnetic crust.

Earth's magnetic field is much stronger and generated by the planet's core. But it too is affected by the solar wind, as scientists call the constant stream of charged particles flowing off the sun and across space. On the side of Earth opposite the sun, the solar wind stretches the magnetic field to form an elongated tail structure, like a terrestrial windsock.

A range of missions have studied this magnetotail at various points up to nearly 800,000 miles (1.27 million kilometers) away from Earth, but beyond that, it has been a mystery. Conveniently, the two ESCAPADE spacecraft traveled through the structure over the course of about 10 days early last year at a much greater distance from Earth — some 1.19 million miles (1.91 million km) away.

"We were able to capture the magnetic field structure of that distant tail, how it flaps, how it moves, how the plasma flows within it," Lillis said, gathering data that he called "unprecedented." "And not only that but for the first time, we were able to do that with two spacecraft simultaneously."

A second shot at the magnetotail came over the summer, when the spacecraft were much closer to Earth but the moon was also passing through the magnetic structure. The two ESCAPADE spacecraft were able to partner with twin ARTEMIS-THEMIS spacecraft at the moon to observe the structure from four different perspectives simultaneously.

That's a huge boon when trying to study the magnetotail and its intricate and ever-changing magnetic field lines and plasma, Lillis said. "These are generally not things you can measure remotely," he said. "You kind of have to be there."

Meghan Bartels is a freelance science journalist based in New York City who worked on staff for Space.com from 2018 to 2023. Her writing has also appeared in outlets including Scientific American, Science News, Wired, Newsweek and Audubon.

Mona Chalabi on Money and Art

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

An interview with the Pulitzer Prize winner, Lori Waxman on bringing sexy back in Chicago, stolen Picasso prints found, and the problem with clouds.

“I wrote this book because I was paid a shitload of money to write it,” Mona Chalabi says in her new book Ten Lives: How What We Have Shapes Who We Are — or tried to, anyway, before her editor stopped her. The Iraqi-British, Pulitzer-winning artist and illustrator has made a career saying what nobody else can, or will — calling out fellow journalists for their refusal to report on Palestine, for instance. Today, she talks to Associate Editor Laskhmi Rivera Amin about her data-driven, hand-drawn process, as well as how class and inequality shape journalism, art, and our lives.

Funny — as an editor, my favorite thing is not stopping my writers. That’s precisely how Lori Waxman’s piece on the return of sexy art in Chicago was born. No, this is not a personal essay about her or her acquaintances’ sexuality (“Maybe after everyone’s dead,” she says). It’s much better — loaded with art historical judgments (has anyone referred to Willem de Kooning’s drawings as “slasher sketches” before? We ought to); discussion of glory holes and sexy architecture; and gems about life (“female middle-age libido sets at either zero or 1,000, on a scale of one to 100”). God, I love my job.

“As an adult and as a journalist, I have stared shamelessly.” That refusal to look away is precisely what data journalist, writer, and artist Mona Chalabi has built her deeply principled career on. Known best for her indelible data visualizations, from the obscenity of Jeff Bezos’s wealth to the Israeli government’s starvation of Palestinians in Gaza and the racial and gender biases in museum collections, the Pulitzer Prize-winning Iraqi-British journalist tackles the subject of wealth and economic inequality in America in her first book, Ten Lives: How What We Have Shapes Who We Are (2026). | Lakshmi Rivera Amin

Lara Saget’s Sculptures Mine the Ancient, Alchemical, and Divine

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

On the way to Lara Saget’s Brooklyn studio, I see a headstone shop, where grave markers are displayed in the window. On the next block, there’s a warehouse, its open garage doors revealing floor-to-ceiling stacks of granite and marble slabs. And inside, her studio is a bit of a quarry itself.

“This is my rock room,” the 37-year-old sculptor says, gesturing to a corner of her studio that looks like a construction site. “This is where I collect all my different little things.” She points to a pile of gray stone, explaining that it is rare Tuckahoe marble, which was used to build the Washington Square arch. When Saget saw that a hotel was set to be built on a former Tuckahoe marble quarry in Westchester County, she called the owner and asked if she could come collect some.

This practice of seeing the sublime in nature is at the heart of Saget’s practice: She sees the divine in granite, the sacred in destruction that makes way for creation. These are themes she mines in her first New York City solo exhibition, “and the world came apart,” curated by Sarah Darro, which opened at Trotter&Sholer on November 7. Saget is exhibiting the work after recently receiving her master’s in divinity from the Center for Religion and Advanced Spiritual Studies and becoming an ordained minister. This spirituality deeply informs the work, which is about regeneration, the ancient, and the alchemical.

Lara Saget, The Children and the Tree That Turned to Metal, 2026. Copper alloy and petrified wood. 11 x 6 x 3 in.

The show, which variously brings to mind the celestial sculptures of Lee Bontecou and the arresting imagery of Louise Bourgeois, has two components: wall works made from copper alloy, granite, and black marble that borrow the aesthetics of early cave drawings to depict volcanic maps and tectonic plates; and glass sculptures centered on the ancient Judean date palm, a once-extinct tree scientists were able to regenerate from nearly 2,000-year-old seeds, and the Joshua Tree, which is threatened by climate change.

Saget has a broader interest in legacy and inheritance. As the daughter of the late actor Bob Saget, she inherited not just a famous last name, but also the example from an early age of what it looks like to be an artist. Now, almost five years after his death, a documentary about him is in the works. “He was a true artist,” she says. “He was like jazz, just like this constant creation. Being surrounded by that has made it seem very normal to be constantly creating.”

In 2019, she traveled to India for the first time, spending a month in a small village at Art Ichol, a residency for artists working in bronze and stone. Saget has gone back almost every year since to make her work, which involves a 6,000-year-old process called “lost-wax casting.” She works with a team to carve into stone and use wax to make a print of its surface. She then builds a mold around the wax and stone, and constructs a kind of sweat lodge out of bricks and mud to steam out the wax, leaving a cavity for molten metal to be poured in. Every step of the process is manual.

Saget has had a lifelong interest in spirituality, but it wasn’t until the last decade, when she found the Path of Soul Transcendence, that she began to pursue it more seriously. “Honestly, my whole life, I was looking for something,” she says. “The experience of the path is really surrendering to the spirit that I am, that everybody is, and the reality that everything is one.”

It’s an idea essential to her practice, which is rooted in how materials interact with each other. “I just had the sense that when heat breaks everything down, it's all ultimately one,” Saget says. “The center of the earth, or how volcanoes are also obsidian rock, and how all these materials are basically everything. It seems like it’s totally separate, but when it's heated, it reveals it's not.”

Saget’s wall works take a bird’s-eye view, using a tiled-grid modularity that evokes sites of excavation and discovery. The Last One Million Years (2026), for example, is a map of volcanoes and tectonic plates she charted using a celestial map from NASA. The raised rock parts of the maps are from heat-trapping granite in the metal, a spontaneous interaction. The effect is a kind of spiritual archaeology, wherein Saget uses the earth’s most ancient, essential materials to tap into its profundities.

Lara Saget, Joshua, 2026. Kiln-cast glass and phosphorescent powder, 15.5 × 6 × 6 in.

If the wall works are the story of the last million years, Saget’s tree sculptures are the story of the present and future. She started making glass tree sculptures in 2016, focusing on trees that have survived against all odds. She began sculpting Joshua trees during the pandemic, when she drove across the country to Yucca Valley Material Lab in Joshua Tree for a residency. While she was there, it snowed—a rare occurrence—which made her think about how the Joshua tree survived the Ice Age. Similarly, the sculpture Rebirth of an Ancient Palm is a recreation of the ancient Judean date palm, which was brought back to life from extinction. Both sculptures feature textural, kiln-cast glass using the lost-wax process. For the exhibition, she had two children narrate an audio piece, each acting as one of the trees and encouraging people to take care of them. “Nobody could imagine that these trees could survive all of this,” she says. “It’s really about the underlying life force that continues against all cognitive understanding.”

Indeed, she’s begun to think about grief in similar terms. “Working through what seemed to be finite, and allowing what seemed finite to almost reveal itself as infinite has helped a lot,” Saget says. “Because I realized that nothing is the end of anything. It’s always in the process of becoming.”

“and the world came apart,” runs through December 19 at Trotter&Sholer at 90 Elizabeth Street in New York City.

Once a Filipino Party Isle, Boracay Has Reinvented Itself

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

“Fun and sunshine, there's enough for everyone,” says a neon sign before me at the discotheque Club Tropicana on Boracay, a four-square-mile island at the heart of the Philippines. The lyrics are from a buoyant 1983 song, also called “Club Tropicana,” by Wham!. But tonight's playlist is cribbed from the 2010s. As midnight nears, Jennifer Lopez's voice sounds over the speakers, “If you're a party freak, then step on the floor.” So I twirl under a disco ball with gleeful strangers in bikini tops, cargo shorts, and Havaianas flip-flops, beers and gin and tonics in hand.

The effect is transportive. I'm flung 10 years into the past, back when Boracay was the locus of an exuberant, if at times debauched, nightlife scene on the creatively named White Beach. Crowds raved until sunrise and woke up on some of the world's softest sand. They had budget hostels and three-star inns, 24-hour shops, water sports and boats, and eateries of all stripes to keep them entertained until nightfall. Then came the parties, VIP bottle service, and the voice of Kesha speak-singing over sound systems, “Tick-tock on the clock, but the party don't stop, no.”

The thing is, it did. Boracay had to shed its hedonistic streak after a series of closures curtailed tourism. First there was the government-imposed cleanup in 2018 ordered by then president Rodrigo Duterte to correct rampant overdevelopment and environmental negligence. Then there was the COVID-19 shutdown from 2020 to 2022, when the Philippines was closed to foreigners. Boracay lost an estimated $845 million in tourism revenue in 2020 alone and counted only 260,000 visitors. Compare that with 2017, when it raked in roughly $1.1 billion by welcoming more than 2 million travelers who dug their toes into a White Beach clogged with the debris of multiday concerts and ragers thrown by local hosts and corporate mega-sponsors. Broken bottles, cigarette butts, and waste—plastic and otherwise—came in and out with the tides.

The writer (right) aboard a paraw tour at White Beach

Despite growing up in Manila, about an hour away by plane, I never got to see “old” Boracay before immigrating to the United States in 2003. In the 1990s it was beloved for its rustic milieu and unfussy hospitality, though it was already a resort island with more than 100 hotels. My family knew it best in the '80s, when backpackers from Europe and the US began to arrive after the German writer Jens Peters called it, in his 1978 guidebook to the Philippines, “paradise on Earth.” In those halcyon days, paradise had no electricity or potable water. Ice was brought in from the mainland to chill San Miguel Pale Pilsens in Coleman coolers. Even my stepfather, a reluctant traveler, fondly remembers the Boracay of yore: “Your mother and I had only the sea and the stars.”

Boracay is changing yet again. Since 2022 it has vowed to revitalize its economy without sacrificing its environment. Laws now enforce a maximum of 19,000 tourists per day. Smoking, alcohol, littering, single-use plastics, and parties directly on the beach are forbidden. Visitors are not permitted without reservations at accredited lodgings—no more sleeping overnight on the sand. No longer raw as it once was, neither an unspoiled Eden nor a profligate Sodom, this grown-up Boracay is what I've come to see for the first time. As I gaze at the stars above a pristine White Beach, I think, So far, so good.

“Boracay was stuck in a mode,” says entrepreneur Nowie Potenciano as we have brunch at his restaurant Percy Seafood, part of his beachfront hotel Seaworthy Boracay. He's referring to both the island's party animal years and its old culinary scene. Back when sustainability and dairy alternatives weren't yet part of the vernacular, diners usually packed their own food from the cities or bought fresh fish at wet markets to have them grilled or fried at canteens called paluto, meaning “to have something cooked.” They remain part of Boracay's charm today, but now we also have the dishes on our table like mango and tuna ceviche, seafood tagine with prawns and mussels, and grouper marinated in sambal. Dessert is a mélange of coconut-pandan ice cream, mango cubes, and crispy rice from Coco Mama, another Potenciano venture. The entire treat is vegan, and I, lactose intolerant, am grateful.

A seafood platter at Percy Seafood, right on the beach

Pecho, a popular chicken dish at Island Chicken Inasal, served with frozen calamansi

The owner of multiple Boracay restaurants, Potenciano prioritizes sourcing locally. Mahi-mahi and grouper come from nearby Roxas City, oysters from marshy New Washington, and lobsters from the colorful reefs of Palawan. Last October he opened a new restaurant, Hello, Po, inspired by open-fire cooking and smoking traditions from all over the Philippines. As much as possible the ingredients come from Panay, the main island off which Boracay sits. “I want tourists to realize that Boracay is at the center of so much wealth in terms of food and culture,” he says. “It's not just another beach.”

That said, judging it solely as a beach, I get why Boracay is beloved. The sand is soft like confectioners' sugar. The shallows are so vast the waters are basically a giant kiddie pool. Behind a fence of palm trees, all tall and outstretched, as if they're fighting for a cab, there are countless bars, restaurants, canteens, shops, resorts, and hostels. They're divided into “stations” typified by vibe: Station 3, the backpackers haven; Station 2, the busy commercial hub; and Station 1, the home of high-end hotels. Yet wherever your accredited accommodations may be, you can park a blanket anywhere on White Beach. Everyone's welcome to do nothing but sit here and watch the blazing postcard-perfect sunsets.

But I do no such thing. Having missed out on Boracay's previous eras, I am anxious to play catch-up and pack my calendar with activities. While staying at The Lind Boracay, I board a paraw, a traditional outrigger sailboat, and zip along the shore with guides who balance on the watercraft with surprising grace. I go for a snorkeling safari (stunning) and my first try at paddleboarding (nauseating) at the sumptuous Shangri-La Boracay's private Punta Bunga Beach. In the clamorous whirl of D'Mall in Station 2, I shop at the boutique Hiyas, scoring a watch with a pearl bracelet, then grab lunch at Island Chicken Inasal Boracay for grilled birds marinated in calamansi. I have a cocktail at Exit Bar, where a band plays “Easy” (“Easy like Sunday morning…”). After dark the vibe shifts: A handsome, leathery, maybe Swedish DJ busts out his turntables. As I join the crowd untz-ing to the electro-house beat, a fog seems to roll in. It's smoke from cigarettes and vapor from vapes.

Though it leaves me breathless, this rapid-fire succession of capital-E experiences feels natural, like what one is meant to do here. On Boracay the surf shops, tiki bars, karaoke venues, and all-you-can-eat buffets are arranged like dominoes, each address falling into the next. One night I catch a band of dads singing Filipino love songs at Nigi Nigi Nu Noos, a down-home resto-bar with casitas, before I go to Rainbow Cafe and Lounge, where a drag queen performs to a RuPaul song, leaping from a chair and landing in an earth-shaking split. This cornucopia of options can be overwhelming to some and a boon to many. Travelers, alone or otherwise, choose their own adventures and curate vibes by the hour. After decades of being many things to many people, today's Boracay now has everything everywhere all at once.

More people are on the way. Visitor numbers are back to pre-closure levels and only increasing: 2.1 million travelers arrived in 2025, up 3.7 percent from the previous year. To handle them all, a new passenger terminal at Godofredo P. Ramos Airport in Caticlan, the gateway to Boracay, is meant to open in December. Domestic travelers currently outnumber the big spenders from abroad nearly five to one (the split was 50-50 before 2018). But there's still the sense, among business owners I meet, that Boracay is meant to represent the best of the Philippines—not just to foreigners but to ourselves as well.

That's the goal at Subo, a modern Filipino restaurant that straddles Stations 2 and 3. Its chef-owner, Jian Sacdalan, takes guests on a culinary field trip through the country's diverse regions with vegetable pinakbet from Luzon, pork belly pochero from Visayas, and coconut-rich sulu laksa from Mindanao. Subo gets a lot of, as Sacdalan calls them, “intros”—those being introduced to the Philippines for the first time. Staff instruct visitors to leave their flip-flops and Birkenstocks at the entrance. This is a Filipino house after all, and I feel my heart flutter when I'm made to do the same.

“And we get the balikbayans,” Sacdalan says, smiling. Translated literally: country returners, like me. “They long for our culture and heritage, and they get that here.”

When I have breakfast at True Home Hotel, I'm still wet and sandy from spending the already-warm day on White Beach. As co-owner and chef Peter Montemayor hands me Bacolod-style chorizo pudpud, garlic rice, and eggs, he says it's good I'm enjoying the beach. It's what brought him back here after working at resorts in Southeast Asia and the Caribbean: “I said, if I ever return, there's only Boracay.” Another balikbayan.

But Montemayor thinks not enough tourists are relishing White Beach, especially after all that's happened to clean it up. During the 2018 closure True Home Hotel shrank from nine rooms to three to comply with new regulations that forbid permanent structures within 98 feet of the shoreline. He's critical of the government's execution (locals who had to move residences or close businesses weren't supported financially) but admits, “When the island closed, it healed.”

He laments how today's tourists stay dry in the shade, viewing Boracay only through their cameras, or cram their schedules with shopping malls, kiteboarding, windsurfing, and booze cruises where water slides catapult passengers off a ship's stern. “Sounds like my itinerary,” I say, and he laughs. “The island's magic throughout all its iterations,” Montemayor responds, “is how it rewards you when you stand still.”

I taste that peace at Real Coffee and Tea Cafe, a diner in Station 2 styled like a bahay kubo, a stilt house with a thatch roof and bamboo walls. Nearly every surface is covered in sun-bleached photos of guests dating back to when it opened in 1996. The delightfully sour calamansi muffin, it's said, hasn't changed in 30 years. The place feels like a brush with what my family must have longed for: that placid and sweet vibe attached to a Boracay of the past—a contentedness with the here and now.

Inspired to take a breather, I find a spot on White Beach, use my shirt as a blanket, and make to watch the world go by. Instead I drift into a nap. I get to sleep on the sand after all. When I wake, I don't find I've missed out. There's still fun and sunshine. There's enough for everyone.

The pathway over the koi pond at Shangri-La Boracay

The part of White Beach with fewer folks and the softest sand is Station 1, home to Filipino-owned The Lind Boracay. Most of the 118 rooms have sea views, and some have private plunge pools; the brand will come to on-the-rise Philippine island Coron in 2027. The sumptuous Shangri-La Boracay is set on a secluded corner of the island, informally dubbed Station Zero. The 30-acre, 219-key resort has a spa, four restaurants, a PADI-certified water sports center for everything from diving to kitesurfing, and two beaches: the private Banyugan Beach and Punta Bunga Beach, which it shares with sleek Crimson Resort and Spa Boracay and lush Mövenpick Resort & Spa Boracay.

It's easy to fuel up everywhere on the island, especially Station 1: Percy does local seafood dishes; the cocktails at Hello Sailor spotlight Pinoy botanicals; and almost every dessert at Coco Mama is vegan. All are part of the sweet seven-room Seaworthy Boracay. The cozy True Home Hotel and Bistro, with three rooms and an all-day kitchen, is a few doors down. Get a calamansi muffin and an iced latte—there's oat milk—at Real Coffee and Tea Cafe, a 30-year-old oasis amid the hectic vibe of Station 2. In D'Mall, Island Chicken Inasal Boracay serves the titular barbecued bird, one of the region's specialties. At D'Mall's beachside entrance, find Exit Bar for sundowners that become all-nighters. The new Nigi Nigi Too, an eat-drink-and-stay all-in-one, is the latest iteration of a 36-year-old institution. On the border of backpacker haven Station 3 and commercial center Station 2, Subo (meaning “a bite” or “feed”) offers a primer on Filipino regional cuisines. Next door is Boracay Island Brewing Company, with locally made ales on tap.

For a taste of classic Boracay nightlife, hit the dance floors at intimate Club Tropicana, neon-lit Om Boracay, and epic Epic, a storied survivor of the island's closures. If you're looking for a dive-y feel, hit Rainbow Cafe and Lounge for some nibbles and drag shows, and Frendz Resort and Hostel Boracay for a spot of community karaoke (the crowd is young; request some current Top 40).

All of White Beach is yours to claim. Be respectful, do not litter, and leave only footprints.

Brisbane Gets Its Moment in the Sun

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Late one afternoon last November, Ty Simon works the room at Golden Avenue. He laughs with a pair of men in suits, tops off the glasses for a table of women in flower-patterned dresses, squeezes shoulders, and nods and smiles his way across the rest of the floor. Waiters ferry wine and coffee and platters of hummus and salads between the kitchen and the split-level dining space. Sheets of bright Queensland sun pour in through the open walls. It's Tuesday, it's lunch, and the place is full, unrushed, and lovely; it feels good to be here. Golden Avenue opened in the middle of last year as the ninth restaurant from Simon and his wife, Milan-born Biànca Marchi-Simon, through their Anyday hospitality group, which started with the Asian-inflected Longtime and went on to include Agnes, a dimly lit open-flame spot that won Australia's Restaurant of the Year in 2023. When Simon returns to our table, I remark on the hospitality that I've just seen. “Here we take a community approach,” he says. “My goal has always been to make sure that the people who like us, love us.”

The community approach may be the key to his city's success. Brisbane—Australia's perpetually distant-third city—has long been reduced by the Aussies to a “big country town.” (Their words—not mine!) Though a good-natured and happy place, it lacked the natural beauty and trendsetting of Sydney and the cultish urban creativity of Melbourne. For decades Brisbane seemed to succumb to the belief that it could never compete. It lacked personality in its Central Business District (CBD) and had pretty much no public transport. Even geography worked against it: In a state prized for its coastline, Brisbane is nearly 50 miles from a surf beach. Locals would often zip overseas or migrate south, perhaps on the assumption that their ambition could never be realized at home. The big country town endured.

The astounding Kith and Kin, from Queensland artist Archie Moore, which won the Golden Lion at Venice’s 60th biennale, on display at GOMA

Then a shift began. The city council embarked on an ambitious rebranding in 2010 to position Brisbane as globally connected and dynamic, giving it the nickname of New World City (“It was about shedding the idea of being the poor cousin,” Simon says). The magnificent Gallery of Modern Art (GOMA) had opened just a few years before, attracting world-class works from the Pacific and beyond. The council leveraged the city's billion-dollar location, just north of the booming Gold Coast, with its high-rises and killer beaches, and south of the covetable Sunshine Coast, with the Great Barrier Reef just off its shores. Millions traveled in from all over each year for what lay on either side of Brisbane, so why not make the city bait for some of their money and time? Many Brisbanites, Simon included, began to grow this place together, creating a city that reflected their own ambitions—and themselves. In the 2010s the city developed sharply designed precincts filled with glamour shopping and higher-level dining, all under year-round sunshine and with Brisbane's noticeable good cheer and friendliness. In 2021 the IOC announced that Brisbane would host the summer Olympics in 2032, following Los Angeles.

And right around that same time, COVID hit. While the rest of Australia struggled with lockdown and tight regulations that dragged on, Queensland fared generally well, with looser rules and relatively low numbers of cases after the initial shutdowns. “We had Melburnians coming into Brisbane just to feel normal again,” says entrepreneur and developer Catherine Malouf.

Malouf may be the most recognizable face of Brisbane's new chapter. Her family is one of the key forces behind the James Street precinct, a fig-tree-studded melange of open-air coffee houses, Champagne bars, and high-end restaurants strung together with designer shops selling top local labels. “I wanted to create somewhere where we could have a good lunch, a glass of wine, and go shopping in a place that felt like the best of Australia,” she says as we eat grilled chicken and salads at her Hellenika restaurant. Stylish and elegant, Malouf exudes the kind of sophistication you may more often associate with the places she formerly called home, like Italy and California, but her good nature and easy friendliness is all Brisbane. In a sense she personifies the version of Brisbane she is invested in creating: global and chic but anchored in hometown charm. “It took me 20 years to come back and realize that this is the place I want to live in, and a lot of people have realized that too,” she says. “It is comfortable. It is easy.”

The precinct's architecture is heavy, concrete, and borderline Brutalist, but not at all ugly. It creates an aura of strength and permanence that's almost symbolic for the next chapter of a city whose heritage architecture was mostly knocked down and whose locals have often fled elsewhere for opportunity. And amid it all is the anchor of the precinct, The Calile, an urban resort hotel whose execution feels Aussie but whose ambition and hospitality would work in any major global city. Its opening—along with its smartly designed rooms and mezzanine-level pool, bars, and restaurants that spill over into the general precinct sidewalks—catapulted the James Street precinct onto the radars of international travelers. “We are very much an Aussie hotel, but we are increasingly hosting people from around the world,” Malouf says.

Designer Heidi Middleton inside her atelier, Artclub

The James Street precinct serves as a gateway to Australia. The stores here, high-end and, for the most part, independent, are nearly all Australian brands such as P. Johnson, St. Agni, and Brisbane's own Gail Sorronda. A centerpiece is Artclub, from fashion designer Heidi Middleton, which launched here last year. Like many of her fellow Brisbanites, Middleton left to start and grow a business whose ambitions, she felt, would never flourish in Brisbane. After spending decades in Europe and selling her globally renowned Sass & Bide, she alleviated her homesickness and moved back. Artclub is a sustainably minded fashion brand that focuses on small production and renewable materials. Middleton remarks that the key to the precinct's success is the curation: high-end and never high street. “They have created a genre that is all their own,” she says.

Brisbane is called the River City for the coffee-hued eponymous waterway that snakes through it. Like the James Street precinct, the river, too, has been a focus for a lot of redevelopment. The work here—walkways, bridges, cycling and jogging lanes, dining districts, and green spaces—has pulled the city together and been a catalyst for a revamped relationship between people and place. On the banks near the James Street precinct, where a once industrial wasteland lay abandoned and uninspired, now sits the reimagined Howard Smith Wharves, alive with breweries, green parks, and restaurants. It's located on the Brisbane Riverwalk, a network of overwater walkways that are dotted with bright red royal poinciana flame trees, which didn't exist 10 years ago. Along the walk sits Stanley, an excellent Chinese spot that conjures a Hong Kong evening parlor and was opened by Louis Tikaram, who made a name on the global culinary stage as executive chef of EP & LP in West Hollywood. A 15-minute stroll away is Supernormal, the only sister restaurant to the hit Melbourne joint of the same name, whose 2024 opening unofficially announced Brisbane as a serious foodie city.

A signature steamed dumpling basket from Stanley

Four new pedestrian bridges have opened to decrease car traffic, promote sustainable living, and solve some of the city's connectivity issues. The latest, Kangaroo Point Bridge, connects the suburb of Kangaroo Point to the CBD, and it's bookended with coffee bars and has green spaces so that pedestrians can relax while looking out over the water. Zigzagging underneath them all is the beloved CityCat ferry, which zips from bank to bank, chartering passengers throughout town and beyond. Stops include the wonderful South Bank cultural district, where you'll find GOMA and the Queensland Art Gallery (QAG). It chugs its way to West End, Brisbane's perennial counterculture neighborhood, which has a funky retro cinema, indie shops selling Japanese crafts and fashion, and solid food.

Take the ferry north and you'll arrive at Brisbane Powerhouse. A hulking industrial building that powered the city starting in the 1920s, it was reborn in 2000 as the city's contemporary art center, complete with sprawling grounds, rooftop areas, bars, restaurants, and untouched industrial spaces that can host interactive exhibitions. It's raw and cool, and its scale is enormous. When I meet Louise Bezzina there, she is in her second week as the Powerhouse's artistic director and CEO. Kind and unassuming, she is the type of person you might casually meet at a mutual friend's birthday dinner and, 15 minutes into the conversation, realize you are sitting next to someone brilliant. “As Australia develops it allows for more cities to grow—it is not just Sydney and Melbourne,” she says. “Brisbane has long been an untapped city of opportunity.”

She is part of the cohort of local leaders drilling into its potential. Prior to the Powerhouse, Bezzina was the creative director of Brisbane Festival, the largest international festival in Australia by attendance numbers, for which she secured the Jean Paul Gaultier show over creative capitals like Melbourne in 2024. “My edgy style suits Brisbane,” she believes. For her first project at the Powerhouse, she launched “Swingers: The Art of Minigolf,” an interactive statement on female empowerment in a male-dominated world, pegged to the origin story of the putting game, which was founded in the 19th century by Scottish women who had been banned from 18-hole golf on account of their gender; each of the nine holes here was designed by a different female-identifying artist. Bezzina has begun ideating plans to partner with Los Angeles and Pacific dance groups in the lead-up to (and handoff of) the Summer Olympic Games.

But to suggest that creativity is new to Brisbane would be misleading. Designer Lydia Pearson is a local loyalist who has long believed in the power of her hometown. “It used to be that if you wanted to be successful, you had to either leave or create it yourself,” she says. She chose the latter. In 1989 Pearson cofounded the fashion brand Easton Pearson with fellow designer Pamela Easton, which focused on circular fashion through textiles and hand embroidery. It took off, showing during Paris Fashion Week in 1999 and selling in 20 countries before the label closed in 2016. The following year the Museum of Brisbane acquired more than 3,000 pieces from the Easton Pearson archive and has put some of the items on permanent display. “The hard part was getting people to believe you could make a brand in Brisbane that wasn't simply a hibiscus-patterned dress,” Pearson says.

We meet in the leafy suburb of Red Hill, where she and her current partner, the artist Shilo Engelbrecht, work diligently to upcycle old fabrics into a new life through their label, ShiloLydia. This could mean turning an antique tablecloth into a sheer, soft dress, or reworking discarded linens into sharp shirts whose silhouettes could compete with any on a global catwalk. The work is slow, careful, and considered—a preservation effort as much as it is a creative one. The results are delicate and beautiful, a quiet and enduring counter to the louder creativity happening across the city.

Their studio sits inside a Queenslander, an old, iconic style of house that started to appear in Brisbane in the mid-1800s. The structures are wood, often colorful but always fragile, with large verandas designed to catch and route cooling breezes through the home. Queenslanders are delightful and charming, and have become recognizable to millions of viewers around the world who tune into Brisbane's most famous export, Bluey. The beloved animated show about Bluey, a blue heeler dog, who is likely known to more people around the world than her real-life fellow Brisbanite Jacob Elordi, is the creation of animator Joe Brumm. The show airs in 28 languages and more than 140 countries. For several years it was the most streamed show in the United States of any genre, and it has an empire worth $2 billion. Brisbane is a main character, and the city is unmistakable via the iconic Queenslander wood homes, the ibis birds pecking around, the sun and steep hills, the hours of imaginative outdoor play that Brumm says is modeled after his own upbringing in Queensland. Bluey resonates with parents and children of the world due to the heart and the humor at the show's core, something Brumm says defines Brisbane. “It is about the cheer that I feel when I come back,” he says. A good reminder, then, that a place's most resonant selling point is often something that can't be built.

True urban resort The Calile Hotel helped put Brisbane on the traveler map. The hotel has a phenomenal pool and fitness space; even if you're not staying here, swing by for its Lobby Bar and excellent restaurants, including Hellenika, which serves Greek favorites.

Inside a restored heritage Queenslander, the intimate Spicers Balfour Brisbane is well-located in New Farm: It's walking distance from the James Street precinct, the Howard Smith Wharves, and Brisbane Powerhouse.

The dimly lit, decidedly grown-up restaurant Agnes, which was named Australia's Best in 2023, is known for its exceptional wood-fired cooking of duck, steak, and more.

Housed inside a former church in West End, August is a nod to the reverence of Sunday lunch, with considerately prepared plates of Noosa snapper and Jerusalem artichoke risotto.

Sleek and light-flooded, the 15-month-old Middle Eastern spot Golden Avenue has great eggplant and grilled meats.

Do an outdoor table and load it up with all the dumplings and noodles you can order at superb riverside restaurant Stanley that feels like high-end Hong Kong parlor dining.

The Brisbane outpost of the hot Melbourne restaurant Supernormal has a glamorous dining room with large water-facing windows and a fun, Asian-inflected menu that leads with local produce and protein.

Located near the funky Fish Lane, Southside is the spot for dumplings and siu mai after strolling the museums of South Bank.

The French Exit, the newest venture from Ty and Biànca at Anyday, has Parisian-style interiors by star designer Tamsin Johnson and cool bars and dining rooms spread over multiple floors in a heritage building.

Brisbane's cultural hub is South Bank, with Gallery of Modern Art (GOMA) and other institutions featuring Indigenous, Australian, and Pacific Island artists. The riverfront Brisbane Powerhouse has ambitious and edgy festivals, exhibitions, and events throughout the year.

For the best Australian labels, head to the James Street precinct for objects from Dinosaur Designs; stylish dresses from St. Agni; and sustainable, one-off fashions from Heidi Middleton's atelier, Artclub. Over in Red Hill, drop into ShiloLydia as well as the other ateliers that share space in the same Queenslander. Shilo Engelbrecht also hosts collage and meditation workshops in her studio out back by appointment.

The best way to see (and travel) Brisbane is the CityCat ferry, which bounces between 22 stops along the river. But the city has made walking easier too. Stroll the overwater footpaths on the waterfront up to the gorgeous City Botanic Gardens. If you‘re keen for a longer walk after checking out the exhibitions at GOMA, find your way to West End for funky vintage shopping and phenomenal food.

There is no shortage of worthy weekend getaways from Brisbane, including the Gold Coast and Noosa on the Sunshine Coast. But drive an hour inland to the artsy, beautiful Tamborine Mountain for a lovely look at a lesser-explored side of Queensland. Stay at the chic, two-year-old Tamborine Boutique Hotel, where a pantry and bar stock local snacks. Have lunch at the petite Picnic Real Food Bar, and don't worry if it's already run out of the plump and delicious pies (everything is worth trying). Then head around the corner for local amaro tastings at Loborn. Restore yourself after mountain hikes at the floating Sol Elements spa, whose soaking baths and salt room all seem to hover over a lake.

We understand that time is the greatest luxury, which is why Condé Nast Traveler mines its network of experts and influencers so that you never waste a meal, a drink, or a hotel stay wherever you are in the world.
