A Tribute to Bunker Roy: The Man Who Turned Village Grandmothers into Solar Engineers

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Sanjit “Bunker” Roy, who taught illiterate grandmothers to become solar engineers, passed away peacefully this morning at his home in Tilonia, Rajasthan, aged 81.[1] I first encountered his work not in India, but in the rain forests of Borneo.

It was October 2016, and as night fell, the surrounding Borneo tropical rain forest had come alive with noise, dominated by the trill of frogs and the rhythmic pulse of cicadas. I was about to embark on the Sabah study tour of the Emerging Leaders Dialogue Asia (ELDA). Over the next two weeks, we would visit timber plantations, mountain communities recovering from a deadly earthquake, and marine parks.

But undoubtedly the highlight for me was discovering the inspiring story of Barefoot College, which trains village grandmothers to be solar engineers. On our first evening, while enjoying a mini feast of banana fritters, pomelo, and langsat (a bit like lychees but more delicious), we were told about the college’s remarkable partnership with the Sabah Women Entrepreneurs and Professionals Association (SWEPA) in Borneo.

Barefoot College was the brainchild of Bunker Roy, and he was an unlikely revolutionary. Born into privilege in Bengal in 1945, he was educated at the Doon School and St Stephen’s College, Delhi—two of India’s most elite institutions—and was a national squash champion. A conventional career beckoned. Then, during the Bihar famine of the mid-1960s, he saw rural poverty firsthand, and his life changed course.[2] When he told his mother he wanted to live and work in a village, he later joked, she “went into a coma” and didn’t speak to him for a long time.[3]

In 1972, Roy settled in Tilonia, a dusty village in Rajasthan, and founded the Social Work and Research Centre (SWRC), which later became known as Barefoot College. Its rules turned conventional education on its head. Anyone with a university degree was disqualified from studying there, and no degrees were awarded. In 2011, Roy said that nobody at the college earned more than $100 a month. The campus itself was built in 1986 by a dozen local “barefoot architects” who could not read or write, and it went on to win an Aga Khan Award for Architecture. At the college’s night schools, children aged 6 to 14 elect their own prime minister and cabinet.[4]

Roy’s guiding conviction was simple: “There is no urban solution to a rural problem.”[5] He was awarded the Padma Shri, one of India’s highest civilian honors, in 1986, and named one of Time magazine’s 100 most influential people in 2010.[6] He is survived by his wife, Aruna Roy, the former civil servant who led the grassroots movement behind India’s Right to Information Act.[7]

The positive impact of his work has been immense. By the college’s own count at its 50th anniversary in 2022, it had trained 1,708 illiterate and semi-literate rural women from 96 countries as solar engineers, bringing light to more than a million people in over 75,000 households and saving about 45 million liters of kerosene. Its rainwater harvesting had captured some 70 billion liters and brought safe drinking water to more than 1,650 schools. More than 90,000 working children had studied in its night schools, and its barefoot health workers had treated over 300,000 patients.[8]

During our study tour, SWEPA arranged for us to meet one of Barefoot College’s beneficiaries, a so-called solar mama. She was part of the program that takes illiterate grandmothers from poor rural villages and, within six months, trains them at the college in India for their new job. They return to their villages to install and maintain full solar lighting systems. Roy deliberately chose grandmothers because they are rooted in their communities; men and younger people, he found, were more likely to take their new skills and leave for the city.[9] Because the women shared no common language, much of the training was done through sign language and hands-on practice.[10]

Malaysia’s first solar mama—very likely the woman I met that evening—was Tarihing Masanim, from Kampung Sonsogon Magandai, near Kota Marudu in Sabah. She completed her training in India in 2015 and returned to bring electricity to around 100 homes in her village.[11] Roy loved to tell stories like hers. One of his favorites was about a 55-year-old Afghan grandmother who electrified 200 houses and set an Afghan engineering department head straight on the difference between AC and DC.[12]

The story of the solar mamas captures many elements of what it means to harness the power of responsibility to create a more inclusive society and economy. So many barriers are being broken through, as these amazing women challenge stereotypes about age, gender, and rural capacity. Barefoot College shows that sustainable development—including adopting sustainable technologies—is an option for people living with low incomes in remote villages. The Barefoot model empowers people to be agents of their own destiny.

Bunker Roy stands out as an inspiring example of inclusive leadership. His impact, which is so strongly evidenced by the solar engineers program, is as much about restoring dignity to those who are traditionally excluded as it is about developing or transferring practical skills. Roy, who said he had “a very elitist, snobbish, expensive education in India” that “almost destroyed” him, explained:

“So we started the Barefoot College, and we redefined professionalism. Who is a professional? A professional is someone who has a combination of competence, confidence and belief. A water diviner is a professional. A traditional midwife is a professional. A traditional bone setter is a professional. These are professionals all over the world. You find them in any inaccessible village around the world. And we thought that these people should come into the mainstream and show that the knowledge and skills that they have is universal.”[13]

Roy ended that talk with a line often attributed to Mahatma Gandhi, whose principles guided his life’s work: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, and then you win.”[14] For the solar mamas of Barefoot College, and for the more than a million people whose homes they have lit, Bunker Roy won.

This article draws from content in Wayne Visser’s book Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

Kevin De Bruyne to Everton is on, with the Manchester City legend struggling at Napoli

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Everton have launched a shock bid for former Manchester City man, Kevin De Bruyne.

The Belgian is a legend of the Premier League, having won six titles and racked up an in while racking up almost 200 goal involvements in 288 appearances in the English top flight – but since leaving the Etihad, things haven't been as rosy for him in Italian football.

Now, Everton are prepared to offer the 35-year-old a return to the northwest of England in one of the biggest-name moves of the Toffees' recent history.

De Bruyne spent 10 years at Manchester City before swapping one blue shirt for another and signing for Napoli last summer.

The playmaker has struggled to make a difference in Serie A, however, with Calciomercato reporting that he “appears to be in a decline mentally and could sensationally return to the Premier League in January.”

The report notes that Everton are making a sensational swoop to bring De Bruyne back to England and have already made contact with the player's entourage.

The Merseysiders are up to sixth in the Premier League at the moment and enjoying a good season, despite leaving themselves short over the summer in terms of transfer business.

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Fans were disappointed when the club sold striker Beto without a replacement – leaving Thierno Barry as the only recognised No.9 at the Hill Dickinson Stadium – while a failure to replace long-serving right-back Seamus Coleman after his retirement has meant that midfielder Merlin Rohl has taken the role on this term.

There has been recent uncertainty regarding the loan spell of Jack Grealish, too, with the England international dropped by manager David Moyes after breaching the club's disciplinary rules when he reportedly skipped training.

Grealish – who is on loan from Man City, incidentally – has very little chance of joining the Toffees permanently now, with some suggesting that his loan spell could be curtailed in January.

De Bruyne's contract runs until 2027, with the Partenopei holding an option for another year.

Whether club president Aurelio De Laurentiis decides to release the player midseason remains another matter.

The 35-year-old is worth €8 million, as per Transfermarkt. Everton travel away to Brentford when Premier League action resumes after the international break.

« Elle est horrible » : beIN Sports présente ses excuses après un dérapage sexiste sur Aryna Sabalenka

La chaîne beIN Sports a été contrainte de présenter ses excuses officielles suite à une remarque sexiste proférée en direct visant Aryna Sabalenka. Lundi 9 novembre 2026, un commentateur a tenu des propos déplacés à l'antenne au sujet de la numéro 2 mondiale, provoquant une vive indignation. Un épisode polémique en marge du tournoi.

L'incident s'est produit le lundi 9 novembre 2026, lors de la première journée des Finales WTA disputées à Indian Wells aux États-Unis. Alors qu'un sujet diffusait des images du shooting officiel de la numéro 2 mondiale, Aryna Sabalenka, un commentateur de beIN Sports, pensant être hors antenne avant la rencontre de la Biélorusse, a lâché en direct : « Regarde, regarde, regarde. Elle est horrible ». La séquence a déclenché l'indignation de téléspectateurs sur les réseaux sociaux, certains internautes interpellant beIN Sports et l'Arcom. Une séquence qui a fait réagir.

Ce dérapage est intervenu le jour même où beIN Sports officialisait l'acquisition des droits de diffusion de l'ATP dès janvier 2028, avec neuf Masters 1000, les tournois ATP 500 et les Next Gen ATP Finals, qui viennent s'ajouter au circuit WTA et au tournoi de Wimbledon.

Interpellé sur les réseaux sociaux suite à la diffusion de ces propos, Florent Houzot, directeur de la rédaction de beIN Sports, a déclaré qu'il « regrette et condamne fermement les propos tenus ». La Biélorusse a remporté son premier match de poule face à Karolina Muchova en deux sets (7-6 [7/2], 7-5) en 1h53 de jeu. Une entame solide sur le court. Ian Hart: Champions League or a trophy for Brighton? - Why not both

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

At 62 I’ve been very lucky to have watched Brighton all over England, Wales and into Europe.

During various holidays I’ve also watched - on the TV - in the US, the Caribbean, Middle East. Last week it was the Far East and this week it was the Gold Coast, Australia at midnight. But why was Sunday’s victory so poignant for me?

Did you know with an ad-lite subscription to SussexWorld, you get 70% fewer ads while viewing the news that matters to you.

Not quite Saul on the Road to Damascus, but it’s another 90 minutes of realisation. It was perhaps prompted by sitting, as with Singapore last weekend, with ‘neutral’ football fans and hearing their opinions of ‘our’ football club.

It reiterates the almost seismic changes the club has undergone since that dramatic afternoon at Edgar Street in May 1997.

However somethings don’t change and I hope they never will, as the Albion’s annual commemoration of Remembrance Sunday was both poignant and fitting.

But then on to the action. The arrival of Brentford and the respective chairman’s shared backstory bubbling under in the background. Whilst not quite the M23 Derby perhaps, it’s a game we now look forward to that little bit extra.

Albion never let Brentford settle and by half time - courtesy of two goals and VAR actually doing it’s job - the Seagulls were effectively home and hosed.

So, if I had to don my mortar board and gown (Google required for some readers) and give a ‘quarter term’ report, what would it be?

Wow! Second in the table and the highest scorers in the top flight. One of the two defeats, Chelsea away, was arguably a game we should have won. And given Chelsea’s sporadic form since, if they played again this weekend, it certainty would be a different result.

Playing with flair and panache to reiterate a soundbite from previous columns, we all just have to enjoy the ride. One thing is for certain, we are in the EPL for at least another 12 months!

It’s just what will that 12 months bring. The much coveted trophy? Hopefully.

But in modern day football, what is the real gauge of success? We all want to see Dunky up on a podium lifting a pot, but what if this form continues for the remaining three-quarters of the campaign, and Albion get that all important Champions League spot?

Whether us old dyed in the wool fans like it or not, financially for the Albion that is far more lucrative than a trophy, bar the league title.

And again whilst trying not to get too carried away, there are also two huge factors coming at us over the hill. Champions League qualification, would almost certainly mean huge changes in the club’s wage structure.

If we don’t pay our squad the ‘going rate’, then rest assured somebody else in our league, and we all know the clubs, will. And naturally we would also have to recruit along with the financial implications that would bring.

Whoever you are, you can’t do the CL on the cheap! But again to reiterate, let’s not get carried away.

Many of us remember domestic football long before the advent of the ‘International Break’, but we go into this particular one in the highest league position we’ve ever been for a said break.

So let’s just enjoy every second of it, and cue dear old Doris Day… Que Sera, Sera…….only 28 games to go!

Rui Pinto’s lawyers in talks with UK authorities over cooperation deal for whistleblower

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

He is willing to assist in exchange for protection

Rui Pinto’s lawyers are in talks with British authorities over a potential cooperation agreement under which the Manchester City whistleblower could help to expose serious financial and sporting crime in the UK.

According to information obtained by the Guardian, the Serious Fraud Office has contacted Pinto after it emerged he was willing to assist British authorities with their investigations in exchange for protection.

William Bourdon, Pinto’s lawyer, who has represented whistleblowers such as Edward Snowden and Julian Assange, told the Guardian that contact had been established. “I can confirm that the process is under way,” he said, without providing further details.

If an agreement is reached, the Football Leaks founder could become a significant source of information in investigations into financial crime involving football, law firms and powerful business figures. Between 2015 and 2019, Pinto collected tens of millions of files containing sensitive information. He may be able to assist the SFO in identifying potential tax offences and other irregularities that would otherwise have gone undetected.

The development comes at a significant moment for the 38-year-old Pinto, with his future under Portugal’s witness protection programme uncertain.

Pinto rose to prominence by exposing some of the football industry’s most closely guarded secrets. These included Cristiano Ronaldo’s tax affairs, plans by Europe’s leading clubs to establish a breakaway European Super League and a series of controversial financial arrangements. His revelations triggered investigations and contributed to legal proceedings involving clubs and players across several countries.

More recently, documents collected and leaked by the whistleblower became relevant to the Premier League’s investigation into Manchester City, which resulted in the club being found in breach of financial rules.

The information gathered by Pinto is in the possession of French authorities, who sought previously to include him in a witness protection programme. His arrest by Portuguese police in Hungary in January 2019 disrupted those plans, although France has maintained contact with him. Between his arrest in Hungary and his extradition to Portugal, French authorities travelled to Budapest and copied the contents of the electronic devices in his possession.

Pinto was informed on 30 September, just as his name had made headlines again after the City ruling, that he would no longer be protected by the Portuguese authorities after six years under the witness protection programme. Although the authorities maintained there was no threat to his life, he was advised not to express opinions on controversial issues, to avoid crowded places and to limit the time spent with family members.

The best of our sports journalism from the past seven days and a heads-up on the weekend’s action

Pinto was given 10 days’ warning, even though a decision had been taken in July, and the ruling’s implementation was suspended after it was established that he had not been given an opportunity to mount a challenge or appeal. He then appealed and is awaiting a final ruling.

In 2023 Pinto was given a suspended four-year prison sentence in Portugal for attempted extortion and cybercrime offences. He was acquitted in a second case involving similar charges two years later. The public prosecutor’s office and Benfica, one of the clubs hacked by Pinto, appealed against the ruling. A decision from the higher court is awaited.

Supporters regard Pinto as a whistleblower who cast light on corruption, whereas detractors argue that his tactics breached legal standards and moral principles. His disclosures reached far beyond football. Details involving magistrates, legal professionals and public officials were made public and he served as the whistleblower behind Luanda Leaks, which exposed the inner workings of the business empire of Africa’s richest woman, Isabel dos Santos. She denied corruption allegations.

The Most Expensive Artworks by Pablo Picasso Ever Sold at Auction

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Over the course of seven decades, Pablo Picasso maintained prolific periods of artistic output that cemented his status as the one of most coveted artists of all time. The modernist’s most prized paintings derive from various stages in his life: the early-career Rose and Blue periods and his groundbreaking experiments in Cubism, through his output in the years leading up to and during World War II up to the late 1960s, when he was already world famous.

Central to Picasso’s oeuvre are portraits of his various muses: Marie-Thérèse Walter, Dora Maar, Françoise Gilot, and Jacqueline Roque, each of whom the artist had a series of complicated affairs. These portraits, which range from sensual to tormented, have become some of the most recognizable in art history.

In the years since his death in 1973, Picasso’s stature as an artist elevated to the highest ranks, with some of his works going for more than $100 million in public auctions and reportedly at even higher sums in private deals. Currently, he remains the top-grossing artist at auction worldwide, raking in $245 million across 3,400 lots in 2020 alone. “He is in many ways the gold standard of the art market,” said dealer Larry Gagosian in an interview with ARTnews. “I think he’ll remain that way for the foreseeable future.” Below, a look at the top auction sales of works by the Spanish master.

Femme assise depicts French artist and model Fernande Olivier, who went with Picasso to a remote village in Spain during the summer of 1909. While there, she posed for a number of paintings. One of the few early Picasso Cubist works still in private hands, Femme assise sold for £43.2 million ($63.4 million) at a Sotheby’s London evening sale in 2016, where it exceeded the $43.1 million presale estimate. A telephone bidder won the painting, which had remained under the same roof for four decades. The anonymous seller had bought it in 1973 at Sotheby’s for £340,000 ($486,000). With many of Picasso’s early Cubist works in museum collections, Femme assise was a rarity on the open market at the time. “There hasn’t been a painting of this importance at auction in potentially a generation,” Helena Newman, Sotheby’s head of Impressionist and modern art in London, said at the time.

This vibrant red portrait of Dora Maar measures only 25⅝ by 21¼ inches. Auctioned by Christie’s New York in May 2015 at the house’s “Looking Forward to the Past” sale, it went for $67.4 million to an anonymous buyer, surpassing its $55 million high estimate.

Consigned by Las Vegas casino mogul and top collector Steve Wynn, the work was the third-highest sale of the night, which also saw the record-setting sale of Picasso’s Delacroix-inspired Les femmes d’Alger Version “O” for $179.4 million (see below). Before Buste de femme was acquired by Wynn, it had passed through the hands of the artist’s granddaughter Marina Picasso and Geneva modern art dealer Jan Krugier.

Bearing a second painting on the reverse, the rare double-sided canvas La gommeuse was painted by Picasso when he was only 19 years old. The artist created the picture while grieving the death of his friend and fellow artist Carles Casagemas, who had recently died by suicide—a tragedy that ushered in the Blue Period.

The work sold for $67.45 million (including buyer’s premium) at Sotheby’s New York in November 2015, though it was hammered in below its presale estimate of $60 million. The painting’s seller, American billionaire Bill Koch, had purchased it for $3 million in 1984 at auction. It wasn’t until 2000, while the work was being conserved, that a hidden portrait mocking Picasso’s friend and patron Pere Mañach was discovered on the reverse.

Before Koch owned it, La gommeuse had passed through the hands of esteemed French art dealer Ambroise Vollard, New York dealer Lucien Demotte, and Austrian-American film director Josef von Sternberg, who bought it in 1935 and sold it in 1949 at Sotheby’s for $3,600.

Picasso created his famed Guernica and The Weeping Woman in 1937 amid the tumultuous years of the Spanish Civil War. Painted the same year, Femme au béret et à la robe quadrillée features a more joyous image, that of his muse Marie-Thérèse Walter in a blue, red, and white outfit set against a purple background.

The painting made its auction debut during a Sotheby’s Impressionist and modern art sale in London. At that February 2018 sale, the work fetched £49.8 million (approximately $68.7 million); the winning bidder was Harry Smith, the London-based chairman of the art advisory firm Gurr Johns, who purchased it along with three other Picassos from the sale. It had changed hands only once before that auction: After Picasso’s death, his estate sold it to an unknown private collector.

Picasso’s Femme nue couchée depicts his muse Marie-Thérèse Walter reclining in a state of sensual abandon. The fluid brushwork, vivid contrasts of color, and radically distorted biomorphic forms lend the female body an almost sculptural presence. The Château de Boisgeloup in Normandy, where the painting was created, provided a studio for Picasso’s experiments with form and three-dimensional work.

The painting remained in Picasso’s collection until his death, then with his heirs until 2008, when it entered a private collection. Offered publicly for the first time, it headlined Sotheby’s Modern Evening Auction in New York on May 17, 2022. With an estimate reported at approximately $60 million, it achieved $67.5 million including fees; the hammer price was $58.5 million. The result made it the evening’s top lot.

Hidden from the public for almost 40 years, Dora Maar au chat portrays Picasso’s muse seated in a chair with a black cat on her shoulder. The painting was first acquired by Paris dealer Pierre Colle in 1946 and was later owned by Chicago collectors Leigh and Mary Block.

In 2006, Chicago’s Gidwitz family decided to auction the painting at Sotheby’s New York, where it sold for $95.2 million, almost twice the $50 million low estimate. Sotheby’s granted the family a $53 million guarantee in order to secure the painting, a rarity at the time.

At the evening sale, five bidders competed for the work. Reportedly among them were casino magnate Steve Wynn, Ohio retailer Leslie Wexner, and Microsoft cofounder Paul Allen. But the portrait of Maar went instead to an eager bidder in the room wielding a paddle with the number 1340, in an unusually visible move for a transaction of that level at a marquee auction.

The sale drew speculation as to the identity of the winning bidder, with many suspecting that he was an ultrawealthy collector from Russia. Ultimately, it was revealed that the anonymous floor bidder bought the work for the Georgian mining tycoon Bidzina Ivanishvili. New to the art world at the time, Ivanisvhili, who would go on to serve as Georgia’s prime minister, has a modern and contemporary art collection worth $1 billion, most of which he keeps in storage in London, with replicas of his masterpieces adorning his Tbilisi residence.

Leading Christie’s 20th-century art evening sale in New York in May 2020, Picasso’s Femme assise près d’une fenêtre (Marie-Thérèse) from 1932 sold for $103.4 million, almost double the $55 million pre-sale estimate. After a 19-minute long spar between six bidders, the painting finally sold to a bidder on the phone with Vanessa Fusco, Christie’s co-head of the 20th-century art evening sale, in a moment that proved the art market had returned in full force after a period of economic lag caused by the coronavirus pandemic.

Before its sale at Christie’s, it was last exhibited at Paris’s Picasso Museum showcase “Picasso 1932, année érotique” in 2017, alongside mega-collector Steven Cohen’s 1932 Picasso portrait Le Rêve, which he purchased for $155 million in 2013. The following year, Femme assis went to the Tate Modern in London for an exhibition titled “The EY Exhibition: Picasso 1932, Love Fame Tragedy.”

The work is notable both for its large size, and in its unusual depiction of Picasso’s muse, Marie-Thérèse Walter, who is often portrayed as sleeping or dreaming. Here, she is sitting upright in a chair, as if enthroned with her gaze directed at the viewer. The most striking aspect of the piece, Fusco told ARTnews ahead of the sale, is “the incredible presence of this woman and the agency which he’s given her, which you don’t always see in his depictions of her.”

Picasso’s painting of a seated Parisian teenager smoking a pipe and wearing a crown of roses is a signature work from the artist’s Rose Period and it is one of the works from this era that remains in private hands.

In May 2004, the work was offered at an evening sale in New York by Sotheby’s, alongside 33 other pieces from its owners Mr. and Mrs. John Hay Whitney to raise funds for the Greentree Foundation, the family’s charitable organization. The anonymous bidder, who purchased it for $104.1 million (well above the $70 million pre-sale estimate), beat out six others who were vying for it that evening.

The Whitneys had purchased it from a Zurich-based dealer for around $30,000 in 1950. The buyer is believed to have been Guido Barilla, the billionaire heir and chairman of the Barilla pasta company. When it sold in 2004, Garçon à la pipe became the most expensive ever sold at auction, beating out Van Gogh’s Portrait of Dr. Gachet (1890), which had sold for $82.5 million in 1990. Garçon à la pipe held that record until May 2010.

In May 2010, the star lot of an evening sale of Impressionist and modern art at Christie’s New York was Picasso’s Nude, Green Leaves and Bust, from his 1932 series of paintings of Marie-Thérèse Walter. During the sale, eight bidders competed for the work, driving the hammer well past the $70 million expectation. It ultimately sold for $106.5 million to a phone bidder.

Held in private hands for 60 years, the painting was sold by Los Angeles philanthropist Frances Lasker Brody, who had purchased it with her husband, Sidney F. Brody, from New York dealer Paul Rosenberg in January 1951 for $17,000.

The sale of Peggy and David Rockefeller’s art collection at Christie’s New York, which included top lots by Paul Gauguin, Edouard Manet, Henri Matisse, and Claude Monet, set a historic high for a single-owner sale in 2018. The piece that fetched the highest price was Picasso’s portrait Fillette a la corbeille fleurie, made during the artist’s Rose Period.

The work’s first owners were Leo and Gertrude Stein, the American siblings and early patrons of modern art, who bought it from a Paris dealer the year it was completed. After Gertrude Stein’s death, the painting was passed to her companion, Alice B. Toklas, whose estate sold it in 1968 to a syndicate of collectors associated with the Museum of Modern Art in New York. David Rockefeller then purchased it for himself.

When the painting sold for a staggering $115 million during the blockbuster Rockefeller auction in 2018, its price wasn’t the only reason the piece attracted attention. Featuring a nude prepubescent girl, wearing only a necklace and holding a basket of red flowers, the work generated controversy over Picasso’s sexualized image of a child, known among historians to be an underage flower seller and sex worker named Linda.

The work has long been considered a masterpiece, though. “She represents the themes that Picasso would wrestle with for his life—love, sex, beauty, tenderness, violence,” said Marc Porter, chairman of Christie’s Americas. Since the record sale of the work, criticism among scholars surrounding Picasso’s treatment of women has persisted. In 2021, protesters staged a demonstration outside Barcelona’s Picasso Museum in an attempt to bring attention to the issue.

In La Femme à la montre, Marie-Thérèse Walter appears elegantly dressed, wearing a wristwatch and sitting in an armchair against a blue background. The painting features bright colors, geometric shapes, and a harmonious composition. Her affair with Picasso remained secret for years because the artist was married to Olga Khokhlova. The wristwatch may symbolize the passage of time and the complexity of their romance.

In 1966, collector Ernst Beyeler purchased the painting directly from Picasso’s studio in Mougins, France. Two years later, Emily Fisher Landau acquired it. It remained a centerpiece of her collection for nearly 50 years, displayed above the fireplace in her Manhattan residence until her death in 2023. On November 8 of that year, Sotheby’s auctioned the painting in New York for $139.4 million, making it Picasso’s second-most expensive artwork sold at auction, after Les Femmes d’Alger (Version “O”).

In 1956, the legendary Picasso collector Victor Ganz put in an offer to buy all 15 pieces in Picasso’s Delacroix-inspired “Les Femmes d’Alger” series (1954–55) from Paris dealer Henry Kahnweiler for $212,500. In years to follow, Ganz kept just five from the group after selling the rest of them to recoup his purchase price; Version “O” was among the few he held on to.

Decades later, in 1997, Christie’s staged the blockbuster sale of Ganz’s prized Picasso collection in New York, where the piece went for $31.9 million to London dealer Libby Howie, who is said to have purchased it on behalf of a buyer in Europe. Nearly two decades later, at Christie’s New York in May 2015, Version “O” resurfaced on the market and set a record price of $179.4 million, making it the most expensive work to sell at auction at the time. Reported to have been purchased by the Qatari royal Al Thani family, that painting remains the most expensive Picasso ever to be auctioned.

Primary Architects crowns carpenter's studio in rural China with undulating timber roof

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Chinese studio Primary Architects has topped a carpenter’s studio in China with a sinuous timber roof that responds to the local mountain terrain.

Located at the entrance of Haotang Village, Xinyang, in China’s central Henan Province, the building belongs to Zhang, a local carpenter.

It was designed by Primary Architects to incorporate his studio, as well as a woodcraft workshop that is also open to the public.

Primary Architects conceived of the project, called the Carpenter’s Home, as a lightweight timber structure nestled between the mountain and the village and aligned with the natural elevation of the terrain.

The 405-square-metre building sits at the foot of the mountain and is topped by a roof made up of hyperbolic curved beams that span up to 26 metres.

This sloping roof appears to gently descend from the mountainside and reconciles the elevation difference between the mountain and the village.

“The building responds to the rolling mountain terrain naturally by treating its roof as a gentle extension of the mountain ridge stretching into the village,” Primary Architects explained.

According to the studio, its undulating outline not only echoes the surrounding mountain terrain, but also forms a structural system that enables a flexible arrangement of internal spaces.

The roof’s structural system, which is comprised of glued timber and steel joints, was created through parametric control and digital modelling.

Prefabricated in a factory, the modular roof was assembled on site to improve construction efficiency.

Inside Carpenter’s Home, the ground floor houses the carpenter’s own woodworking shop as well as an area that is open to tourists and visitors, where exhibitions and tours are held.

A mezzanine floor has a tea room for visitors. Like the rest of the space, it is lit by a series of curved skylights that were incorporated into the timber-structured roof to introduce natural light into the interiors.

The angle of each skylight was carefully positioned in accordance with the movement of the sun to allow the interiors of the studio to be well-lit during working hours.

The open interiors were designed both to accommodate the traditional wood-working practice and to function as an education centre for the craft.

An expansive staircase that doubles as seating connects the studio space on the ground floor to the mezzanine tea room.

From here, visitors can observe Zhang’s working process, as well as his tools and material samples displayed on the side walls.

Primary Architects also created a series of working benches that can be combined and divided to support various aspects of the carpenter’s work.

“The Carpenter’s Home seeks to break through the existing formal paradigms of rural construction, responding to the land, craftsmanship and daily life in a more imaginative way,” said Primary Architects.

“With contemporary timber structure as its medium, it brings traditional woodworking back to rural daily life, making the building not only a space for accommodating activities, but also a continuously unfolding lifestyle,” it continued.

“For Haotang Village, it is not only a craft workshop, but also a timber vessel embodying local memory, artisan spirit and the vision of the future countryside.”

Primary Architects is a Beijing-based architecture studio founded by Xiang Huang in 2020. This project was shortlisted for workplace project (small) category at this year’s Dezeen Awards.

Elsewhere in China, Büro Ziyu Zhuang designed a museum cluster to “blend seamlessly” into a meadow, while RSHP completed a skyscraper in Shenzhen with a “striking butterfly layout”.

Chief architects: Xiang Huang, Songyan Mao Architectural design team: Cong Liu, Zhiyan Lin, Xiaoxia Wang, Junjie Liu, Xiaofeng Zhang, Yao Lu Grade A design institute: Zhongke Architectural Planning and Design Research Institute                                                                         Wood structure detailed design: Bowen Li Construction company: Shanxi Junchen Construction Co., Ltd. Client: Xinyang Pingqiao District Juxing Agricultural Investment and Development Co., Ltd. Building area: 405 square meters

"Why Is It Not Fun?" - Doug Bowser Gives A Peek Into Nintendo's Strategy Sessions

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

It's no secret that when it comes to game development, Nintendo is all about fun. We all know this firsthand, of course, but it's still interesting to get a peek behind the curtain and find out how this mission statement works its way into development itself.

At the recent GamesBeat Next 2026 event, former Nintendo of America President Doug Bowser provided exactly that. Joining fellow former industry higher-ups Peter Moore and Shawn Layden on the 'Game Legends' panel, Bowser discussed how Nintendo's ultimate objective, "We make smiles", carries through into game development and internal reviews.

Bowser recalls sitting in on a strategy session with legendary Nintendo producer Shinya Takahashi, in which the dev wasn't interested in how the testers were playing the game, but rather how the game was affecting the testers. "He wanted enough people in the room that you could see their faces and see their eyes, but he didn’t want any more than that," Bowser said. "He would sit there and literally watch your expression. He never watched what your hands were doing on a controller. He never watched what was happening on the screen. He watched your eyes and your facial expression."

This would continue into session feedback. "He wanted to see, are you having fun?," Bowser continued. "If he didn’t see it, he would ask you directly. 'Why is it not fun?' That was direct feedback that you got."

It all sounds like a pretty strenuous ordeal (there's no magic dev button to 'add fun', after all), but hey, it clearly works for Nintendo — the house behind some of the funnest games in history.

Even though Bowser is no longer heading up NoA, having been replaced by Devon Pritchard earlier this year, he said to the panel that this smile-first approach is still important today, particularly considering new generations' needs "are fundamentally different than ours are."

Pointing to the likes of Fortnite, Roblox and Minecraft, Bowser argued that younger gamers "do want fun, and they want it to be easy to get to fun," but it's increasingly about self-expression in gaming. "It’s important for us to keep an eye on those cohorts as we build our next games."

Elsewhere in the panel, the former game leaders discussed AI in game development (something they all seem rather positive on), the console wars, exclusives and more. You'll find the full transcript on Games Beat.

Crystal Palace are reportedly weighing up a managerial change, with former Tottenham Hotspur boss Thomas Frank emerging as a leading candidate to take charge at Selhurst Park.

The Eagles have endured a disappointing start to the Premier League campaign, and the club’s hierarchy is increasingly concerned about their performances under Pierre Sage. According to reports from NY Times, Frank is among the managers Palace is considering as they assess options ahead of a potential change in the dugout.

The Danish manager could be an attractive option for Crystal Palace as they look to bring stability to the team. Frank enjoyed considerable success at Brentford, guiding the club into the Premier League and helping them establish themselves in the top flight.

His experience in English football could prove valuable for Palace, who need someone to improve their defensive organisation and get the best out of their existing squad. However, his spell at Tottenham was less successful, and he will be keen to rebuild his reputation after leaving the north London club earlier this year.

Palace have collected just seven points from their opening 10 league matches, winning twice and conceding 24 goals. Their recent 3-1 defeat against Liverpool was another setback, leaving them at the bottom of the table and increasing the pressure on Sage.

The Eagles have also struggled to maintain defensive consistency, and their inability to address set-piece problems has become a major concern. A managerial change could give the players a fresh start, although the club will need to make the right appointment.

Frank is not the only experienced manager being considered, with Sean Dyche and Vitor Pereira also mentioned as potential alternatives. Dyche, in particular, has considerable experience helping Premier League clubs navigate difficult relegation battles.

It remains to be seen whether Palace will finalise their interest in Frank, but the former Brentford manager appears to be a serious contender. A return to the Premier League could give him another chance to demonstrate his managerial qualities.

For Palace, appointing the right successor will be crucial if they want to avoid spending the rest of the campaign fighting relegation.

Why The 401(k) Still Dominates (Even Its Inventor Has Doubts)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Terms and Restrictions Apply Physician on FIRE has partnered with CardRatings and other partners for our coverage of credit card products. Physician on FIRE and CardRatings may receive a commission from card issuers. Some or all of the card offers that appear on the website are from advertisers. Compensation may impact on how and where card products appear on the site. POF does not include all card companies or all available card offers. Credit Card Providers determine the underwriting criteria necessary for approval, you should review each Provider’s terms and conditions to determine which card works for you and your personal financial situation. Editorial Disclosure: Opinions, reviews, analyses & recommendations are the author’s alone, and have not been reviewed, endorsed, or approved by any of these entities.

In 1980, a benefits consultant named Ted Benna figured out how to use Section 401(k), a little-known provision Congress added to the tax code in 1978, to build the first 401(k) plan. It quickly became a favorite among companies looking to offer employees more retirement benefits.

Today, it is still the cornerstone of retirement planning in America. Even physicians who often have a 403(b), 457(b), or defined benefit plan available too, usually max out their 401(k) first.

Recently, Benna has made headlines for calling out his invention, the 401(k), for disproportionately rewarding the wealthy. He may have a point. But I still think the 401(k) is the best retirement account most workers can get, and that it’s still worth it for doctors.

Also read: Beyond the 401(k): Why Physicians Are Turning to Defined Benefit Plans

In a recent Bloomberg interview, Benna argued that the modern 401(k) has grown too complicated and too costly, and that it benefits wealthier savers more than middle- and lower-income workers, the people who arguably need it most.

Affluent employees can afford to max out an account and reap the tax break on top. Benna believes low-income workers either lack access to a plan or can’t afford to contribute if they do.

Research seems to agree. Researchers at Vanguard, Yale and MIT found that about 44% of employer matching dollars go to the top 20% of earners at their companies.

And the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics has reported that while 70% of private-sector workers have access to a defined contribution (DC) plan such as a 401(k), only 49% participate. That means roughly 3 in 10 workers who could use a plan but don’t.

Source: BLS. Alt text: BLS chart showing 70% of private-sector workers have access to a defined contribution plan and 49% participate, March 2026

Discover legitimate, IRS-recognized tax strategies physicians can use to potentially reduce their tax bills, which options high earners qualify for, and which aggressive approaches could attract IRS scrutiny.

Ideal for: Physicians and high-income professionals looking to understand their tax-saving options before speaking with their CPA.

Sat, Nov 14  ·  1:00 pm ET  ·  10:00 am PT

Most physicians sit on the other side of this. Every specialty earns well above the national average. Doximity’s latest compensation report names pediatric infectious disease as the lowest-paying specialty, at $217,819. By comparison, the average American salary is $69,770.

The reason I bring this up here is so that physicians who run practices can make sure their employee 401(k) structures do not reflect these statistics. More on that below.

The 401(k) has been one of the most effective tools for building middle-class wealth the U.S. has ever had.

Household stock ownership went up from 32% in 1989, right before one of the best multi-decade stretches for stock returns on record, to 58% by 2022. Almost one-fifth of American households now hold a net worth above a million dollars— much of it held in home equity and retirement accounts like the 401(k)..

So even if the account rewards people who already earn enough to contribute, it still enables actual wealth-building for millions of ordinary workers who’d otherwise have little beyond Social Security waiting for them at retirement.

Access and participation are both on the rise largely thanks to policy changes. Many states now require employers without a retirement plan to enroll workers in a state-run IRA program. Employers can usually opt out by setting up their own plan, which is pushing more of them to offer a 401(k).

Small businesses, which employ close to half the American workforce, have historically skipped retirement benefits altogether. Tax credits and cheaper providers are changing that. Cerulli Associates projects that the number of 401(k) plans will grow 36% over the next five years, passing one million by the end of the decade.

Auto-enrollment deserves a lot of the credit for the jump in participation.

Under SECURE 2.0, 401(k) and 403(b) plans set up after December 29, 2022 must automatically enroll employees, starting with plan years that began in 2025. Older plans, businesses less than three years old and employers with 10 or fewer workers are exempt.

The effect is large. Vanguard found that plans with auto-enrollment had a 94% participation rate, compared with 64% for plans where employees sign up on their own.

Learn more: Year-End Tax Planning for Physicians. What To Do Before 2026 Is Over

For 2026, the IRS raised the 401(k) employee contribution limit to $24,500, up from $23,500 in 2025. Workers 50 and older can add a catch-up contribution of $8,000 on top of that, up from $7,500, bringing the total to $32,500 for savers in that age bracket.

A newer “super” catch-up of $11,250 lets savers ages 60 to 63 put away even more. It replaces the regular $8,000 catch-up for those years rather than stacking on top of it, bringing their total to $35,750.

re limited to 72,000for2026(80,000 with the age-50 catch-up, or $83,250 for ages 60 to 63), or 100% of eligible pay, whichever is lower. If your plan allows after-tax contributions, the room between your own contributions and that cap is what makes a mega backdoor Roth possible.

Important: starting in 2026, if your wages from the employer sponsoring your plan topped $150,000 in 2025, any catch-up contributions you make must go in as Roth (after-tax) instead of pretax. Two details matter for physicians:

If you’re weighing Roth against pretax for the rest of your contributions, here’s how to choose between a Roth and traditional 401(k). Full details are on the IRS catch-up contributions page.

Many employed physicians don’t have a 401(k) at all. Hospitals and academic centers usually offer a 403(b), which works almost the same way, with the same $24,500 limit and the same catch-ups.

Many also offer a 457(b), which has its own separate $24,500 limit. If you have both, you can defer up to $49,000 in 2026 before counting any catch-ups.

One caution: in a non-governmental 457(b), the money technically belongs to your employer until it’s paid out, so it’s exposed if the hospital runs into financial trouble. Here’s our full guide to 457(b) contributions.

If you own a practice with staff, plan design decides who actually benefits. A safe harbor 401(k) is the common choice. You make a required contribution for employees, either 3% of pay or a match of up to 4%, which vests right away.

In return, the plan skips the annual nondiscrimination tests that can otherwise cap what owners contribute. Under SECURE 2.0, small practices can also claim tax credits of up to $5,000 a year for three years toward startup costs, plus $500 a year for adding auto-enrollment.

Owners who want to save more than the 401(k) allows often pair it with a cash balance plan. And if you’re a solo practitioner with no staff, a solo 401(k) or SEP-IRA is usually simpler.

More than 80% of employers who sponsor a 401(k) offer some form of match, usually tied to how much the employee contributes. Some employers match dollar for dollar up to a set percentage of salary.

Others match 50 cents on the dollar up to a higher contribution threshold. The formula changes how much match each dollar you contribute actually earns, and that’s where dual-income households can come out ahead.

A brief published by the Center for Retirement Research (CRR) examined whether married couples coordinate contributions to capture both employer matches. Just over 40% of couples actively coordinate to maximize their combined match and roughly one in five couples leaves match money uncaptured.

In one example from the brief, a couple decides how to split $480 a month in savings between two accounts with different match formulas. Moving contributions toward the partner with the more generous 1-to-1 match, as opposed to splitting evenly, nets the household an extra $30 a month in employer money.

Stretched over 30 years at a 5% inflation-adjusted return, that $30 monthly difference compounds into roughly $25,000 in additional retirement savings, without either partner setting aside a single extra dollar of their own income.

Source: CRR. Alt text: CRR chart showing how couples split 401(k) contributions to capture employer matches.

Plenty of couples capture both matches without planning it, because each partner contributes enough on their own. That works fine.

Coordination matters when money is tight, like when one spouse is still in residency, working part-time or paying down loans hard. In those years, send your savings to the richer match first, then fill the other.

Say a resident earning $66,000 and her spouse earning $120,000 can save $600 a month between them. Her plan matches 50 cents per dollar on the first 6% of pay. His matches dollar for dollar on the first 4%, up to $400 a month.

Split evenly at $300 each, they collect $450 a month in match. If he puts in $400 and she puts in $200, they collect $500. That’s an extra $600 a year in employer money from the same $600 a month.

Compare match formulas with your spouse, figure out which account pays the better rate, and adjust the split.

Finally, if you’re wondering about how aggressively you should contribute, I’ll say that starting early beats chasing a better return.

Discover legitimate, IRS-recognized tax strategies physicians can use to potentially reduce their tax bills, which options high earners qualify for, and which aggressive approaches could attract IRS scrutiny.

Ideal for: Physicians and high-income professionals looking to understand their tax-saving options before speaking with their CPA.

Sat, Nov 14  ·  1:00 pm ET  ·  10:00 am PT

In an example from Forbes, we see two 25-year-old savers earning identical salaries. One enrolls immediately at a 10% deferral rate with a 3% match. The other waits five years, then contributes 6% to capture the same 3% match.

The second saver’s fund even outperforms the first, returning roughly 8.9% annually versus 8.4%, partly from a private equity allocation meant to make up lost ground.

By 65, the early starter still ends up with roughly $3 million, compared with $2 million for the saver who waited. That $1 million gap comes from two things: five fewer years of compounding and a lower savings rate (9% of pay versus 13%). The higher return didn’t come close to closing it. If you’re still in training, here’s what to set up now.

As you can see, when it comes to retirement savings, your timing often matters more than any other variable.

A dollar contributed at 25 has decades to compound and no later contribution, however large, can fully make up for a late start. The 401(k) is also one of the few accounts you can start on day one without worrying much about what happens when you change jobs.

That’s because, as opposed to pensions and employer-funded bonus plans, the 401(k) is portable. Your own contributions are yours the moment they hit your account, and they go with you when you leave. Employer matches can be different. Many plans vest the match over time, either all at once after up to three years or gradually over up to six.

Leave before you’re vested and you lose the unvested part, so check your plan’s vesting schedule before you time a job change. You can roll it into a new employer’s plan or an IRA, and it will continue to compound without interruption.

Benna may not be happy with how his invention turned out, but for me, looking at how it has created a new class of millionaires, it is the one non-negotiable account in retirement planning for physicians and beyond.

Also read: One Big Beautiful Bill Act: What It Means for Physician Taxes in 2025 and 2026

The IRS caps employee contributions at $24,500 for 2026. Workers 50 and older can add an $8,000 catch-up, and those between 60 and 63 get a larger $11,250 “super” catch-up instead, bringing their total to $35,750.

No. More than 80% of employers who sponsor a 401(k) offer some form of match, but the formula varies. Some match dollar for dollar up to a set percentage of salary, others match 50 cents on the dollar to a higher threshold.

Yes. A 401(k) invests in the market through mutual funds or target-date funds, so balances can drop during downturns. Over decades, though, contributions and compounding have historically outpaced short-term losses for most savers.

It depends. A 401(k) is portable, so you keep the balance and can roll it into a new employer’s plan or an IRA when you switch jobs. A pension pays guaranteed income for life, but most of the benefit builds in your later years with one employer, so people who change jobs often come out behind.

You keep everything you contributed yourself, plus any match that has already vested. The unvested part goes back to the plan.

built99 guide celebrates and archives architectural intelligence of 99 indian practices

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

the built99 guide documents, celebrates, and preserves architectural intelligence from india, revealing how these practices -- and architects -- are progressing the future of our built environment.

What if the architecture in India could lead the world in sustainable, deeply human design? A comprehensive new book highlights this possibility. The inaugural Built99 guide and its companion digital directly, below, explores the future of the built environment by documenting 99 carefully selected Indian architecture and design practices. Rather than simply cataloging past achievements, this expansive volume experiments with an ongoing global dialogue, translating decades of regional research, material ethics, and civic impact into a permanent print edition. The guide examines the foundational, forward-thinking ideas driving each studio’s portfolio.

By elevating these 99 practices onto an international stage on both designboom and ArchDaily, the Built99 guide documents, celebrates, and preserves architectural intelligence from India and the Global South. As the reader turns its pages, the book (see more) reveals how these practices — and architects — are progressing the future of our built environment.

City: Chennai Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

Madras Mise en Scène, Chennai, by 1405 Design Studio, rebuilds a multi-generational Rajasthani family’s 10,000 sq. ft. home on its own former footprint. The studio‘s design draws light and privacy through a tight row house plan that moves from communal ground floors to personal spaces above. A Jodhpur Haveli door, Pichwai and Madhubani works, and brass detailing weave Indian spirit without cliché, while book-matched green quartz rises through the double-height living room. The staircase, with an installation-like prayer bead, pauses inhabitants and guests alike as stage for generations to gather.

City: Punjab Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

Residence Ondra, Amritsar, by 23DC Architects, unfolds as a calm, sun-aligned L-shaped home where courtyards, vertical louvres and glazing balance daylight with comfort. The architects designed a layered arrival sequence — covered parking court, waterbody, pathways and sculptural canopy — that leads toward the foyer, with the pooja room poised above water. At its heart, the double-height ‘Crystal Volume’, a soaring glazed living space anchored by a statement chandelier, connects interiors to landscape, while formal living, dining and kitchen zones flow through sliding openings to the lawn — composed, luminous, and deeply rooted in place.

City: Delhi Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

APL Apollo Corporate Office, Noida, by 42MM Architecture, pairs a bold exoskeletal glass facade with tiered massing that recedes into verdant terraces. A double-height atrium lobby, curved stone and open-plan floors set a refined tone by the studio, flooding interiors with light and collaborative transparency. The executive floor’s triple-height lobby, Sicis flooring and suspended glass meeting room elevate the experience further, while private suites blend classical richness with contemporary polish, which, together, define a new benchmark in luxury corporate design.

City: Kolkata Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

At the Edge of the Thar, Nokha, by Ajay Arya of A Square Designs, sets a Scandinavian-inspired holiday home within Rajasthan’s arid landscape. Local stone, wood and exposed concrete meet a charcoal-and-walnut palette, while expansive glazing frames the desert through a dramatic 24-foot living space anchored by a sculptural indoor tree. The architects positions six bedrooms and a gallery-like circulation to lend individual character, extending outdoors via a plunge pool. Quietly luxurious, the home balances Scandinavian restraint with Rajasthan’s raw, timeless spirit.

City: Kolkata Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

Unscripted, a temporary pavilion at Mumbai’s Architecture & Design Film Festival, curated by Aric Chen alongside an international jury, responds to the city’s dense, restless simultaneity through porosity and interruption rather than resolution. Its structure, created by the design studio, begins as a disciplined grid before fracturing under a 48° shift, carving diagonals that unsettle straight-lined order. Voids cut into the form become thresholds framing light and encounter, where performer and audience exchange roles. Mirroring Mumbai’s own logic, the pavilion finds meaning not in walls, but in the spaces it leaves open.

City: Delhi Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

Airyland Estate, Mussoorie, revives an early-1900s colonial home into a seven-bedroom holiday retreat steeped in Raj-era charm. Restored facades, limestone walls and timber beams merge with reclaimed doors, repolished furniture and local materials, favouring intimate, inviting warmth over minimal modernity while honouring the hill station’s historic character. Overgrown gardens were revitalized with native planting, pathways and mountain-facing seating, blending old and new. The studio’s project stands as a sustainable, sensitive dialogue between heritage and contemporary living.

City: Mumbai Connections: Website | Instagram | LinkedIn | Facebook

Between Ground and Sky, Malappuram, by ADND is conceived as an emotional enclosure where floors ground, walls hold and roofs hover, letting wind, light and monsoon air move freely through. Laterite stone and terracotta-rendered surfaces anchor the house in Kerala’s geology, softened by earthy warmth and texture. The Willow Orchard House, Ladakh, by Alva Architects is built entirely in timber, site soil and hand-quarried granite, without steel or cement, withstanding extremes from +30°C to −25°C. A frost-protected shallow foundation lets the structures nestle gently into the slope among century-old willows. Sun-dried mud blocks, exposed SPF framing and traditional Markalak plaster shape the walls, while solar-oriented planning and heavy insulation retain warmth. Left structurally exposed throughout, the architects‘ building is warm, tactile and quietly engineered.

William Saliba earns Arsenal U21s praise as defender continues comeback from injury

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Lucie Turner writes about Arsenal for Read Arsenal and Newcastle United for Read Newcastle. A long-time Arsenal supporter, she also follows rugby, snooker, darts and Formula 1. Her work covers match previews and reports, breaking news, player profiles and opinion pieces.

William Saliba Arsenal return: William Saliba has made a great impression during his return to action, according to Arsenal’s coaching staff. The U21s manager, David Horseman, praising his attitude and professionalism.

The 25-year-old played the first 45 minutes of Arsenal U21’s 5-2 win at Boreham Wood against Aston Villa on Sunday. Slowly making his way back from his three-and-a-half-month layoff.

Saliba was clearly a quality player, but it was his attitude which impressed Horseman. He worked well with the younger players and was a great example to follow throughout the morning.

The defender had been scheduled to play, and there were no new injury concerns behind his half-time withdrawal. He will return to the first team group as he continues to build up match fitness.

Saliba was praised by Horseman for his positive attitude since joining the club. His preparation, involvement in team meetings and ability to communicate with the younger players stood out.

He also showed his quality with the ball. His passing range helped the Arsenal attack and he made a good weighted pass that led to Marcell Washington running into space before Andre Harriman-Annous’ first goal.

Saliba didn’t see it as below his level to play in the game and really got into helping the academy players.

It set an example for the younger players in the squad. With an established first teamer keeping up the standards, there wasn’t much of an excuse for anyone else not to.

Saliba was not the only senior player to feature, with Marli Salmon as captain and Max Dowman also playing. Dowman, Maalik Hashi and Evan Mooney all scored, and Harriman-Annous added two goals.

It was another key step in Saliba’s return, as he featured in the matchday squad for Arsenal’s game against Slavia Prague after playing 45 minutes in the behind-closed-doors friendly.

He wasn’t selected in the senior squad for their game against Hull City. With only nine subs allowed in the Premier League, Arsenal decided it was best to play him for the U21s.

The decision means that Saliba has been able to play competitive minutes to build up his fitness.

Arsenal will be hoping that he can now make a full recovery from his injury so that Mikel Arteta has another defensive option heading into a busy period for the club.

However, the U21s will have to maintain their high standards from the Aston Villa game without first team players such as Saliba, Salmon and Dowman.

We’ll keep you updated as and when we hear more information about the William Saliba Arsenal return.

Big Walk developer House House reveal genAI use in final stretch of development, only revealing it now as "it has become a matter of public interest"

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Friendslop du jour Big Walk is the latest in an unfortunately long series of games that have been confirmed to use generative AI in their production, with developers House House clarifying their use of it in a statement to Aftermath (thanks!). House House state that "LLMs were used sparingly by House House to help work through difficult technical challenges and bugs", and that "well under 0.1% of Big Walk’s code had anything to do with this usage", but are quick to note that "generative AI was not used in any other context when making the game."

A specific example is given in the case of Unity's apparent weirdness around shadow casters in its rendering pipeline, as it's seemingly difficult to have unnecessary shadow casters behind the player culled, which resulted in the game running at half speed around sunrise and sunset. House House explain that they couldn't find a solution themselves, asked a Unity engineer for help who said it was impossible, and then decided the only answer was to ask an LLM chatbot; "after a few dead ends, it suggested a possible fix, and while we had to modify the suggestion extensively, it worked. This fix represented about 10 lines of code." While absolutely not the most pressing thing to mention here, it is very funny to me for House House to clarify that they "had to modify the suggestion extensively", almost as if this tech isn't actually helpful in giving useful answers.

So, House House touched the stove, despite how easy it is to simply not touch the stove. The thing is, this wasn't a case of being forced to by upper management, as is sometimes the case. This was a choice made entirely voluntarily, and House House are attempting to have their cake and eat it by ending their statement with the reflection that "We have some concern that voluntary disclosures like this might contribute to the normalisation of this technology, or its treatment as “inevitable”. We support the work of those who are trying to limit the harm generative AI is doing to videogames and our world, and apologise that Big Walk’s LLM usage may not meet their standards." But then, you know, that's followed up by saying that Big Walk was developed "without an internal AI usage policy in place, and we consider that lack of policy a failure. We will work to have a more comprehensive policy going forwards." So, not to worry, they learned touching the stove is bad by touching the stove, and definitely won't do it again in future. If only there were thousands of other cases informing them why touching the stove is bad.

Very relevant to this reveal is the recent statement by select jurors of the Independent Games Festival which, after much internal deliberation, came to the conclusion that those who sign the statement would decline to vote for any game that uses generative AI in any capacity, with signees including Austin Walker, Sam Barlow, Lena Reine, Brendon Chung, Aura Triolo, aurahack and more. The statement specifies that "AI-generated code is not an exception to these harms." Fellow juror Liz Ryerson confirmed that Big Walk was being considered by the IGF, and was a matter of discussion in judging as to whether its use of genAI would be acceptable.

As for House House only disclosing this now, they state it was not only because it was necessary for their IGF submission, but also that "we have not made any disclosure on Steam as the platform does not request disclosure of code or chatbot usage", and that they have spoken on it publicly to Aftermath because "it has become a matter of public interest."

If I were to be cynical, I would say that House House would prefer to be the ones breaking this news themselves rather than having the IGF reveal that they had been hiding their use up until now; it's usually easier to control PR if you release it yourself. Big Walk had already been securely in everyone's good books for its support for the BDS boycott, for example, so why ruin that clean rep with something like a genAI disclosure?

I don't know how better to close this article than with an excerpt from that very IGF statement:

"We know that change does not come at the individual level, but as independent developers, we also know firsthand the value of pushing back on what an industry has deemed "inevitable." As jurists, we feel a responsibility to push past defeatism and aspire to a higher ideal.

The future is not yet settled. You are not alone. We can stand together."

FDA Initiates Labeling Changes for GLP-1s Due to Rare But Serious Eye Disease Risk

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

GLP-1 users have a new symptom to watch for: Sudden vision loss in one eye.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) announced on November 9 that it will require drugmakers to add information to the warning labels of Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, Zepbound and other GLP-1 medications about “a potential increased risk” of a rare eye condition called nonarteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION).GLP-1 medications are approved to treat type 2 diabetes and to help people lose weight and keep it off.NAION joins a growing list of side effects linked to GLP-1s, adding a second eye warning to several labels.The U.S. labels for Ozempic, Rybelsus, Trulicity, Mounjaro, and Zepbound already warn about diabetic retinopathy, a diabetes-related eye disease, according to each drug’s prescribing information.The two conditions affect different parts of the eye. Diabetic retinopathy damages tiny blood vessels in the retina, the layer at the back of the eye that senses light, according to the National Eye Institute. NAION damages the optic nerve when it doesn’t get enough blood, according to the FDA’s announcement.Mihail Zilbermint, MD, director of endocrine hospitalists at Johns Hopkins, told Healthline he hasn’t seen NAION in his patients. He has seen vision worsen in some people after they start a GLP-1.“But we believe that’s probably related to rapid improvement in their sugar control, which can also be seen in someone who is starting insulin,” Zilbermint said.

A 2024 study published in JAMA Ophthalmology “initially triggered” the FDA’s review, the agency told Healthline.

The study found a higher risk of NAION in people prescribed semaglutide, the active ingredient in Ozempic and Wegovy, than in people prescribed other diabetes or weight-loss drugs.The authors wrote that more research is needed to determine whether semaglutide causes NAION.Joseph F. Rizzo III, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear in Boston, served as the study’s corresponding author.

In a 2024 podcast, Rizzo said he and a resident saw three patients with NAION who were taking semaglutide for about a week.“And because NAION is a fairly uncommon disorder in the general population, the fact that we had three within a relatively short period of time led me to suspect that there was a higher risk of developing NAION in patients on semaglutide,” Rizzo said. The FDA analyzed clinical trials for multiple GLP-1 drugs and noted that NAION cases were reported with every GLP-1 on the market, according to its announcement. Those findings led the FDA to require the warning for the entire drug class, the agency told Healthline.

About 70% to 80% of people with NAION first notice vision loss when they wake up, according to StatPearls. The FDA listed these symptoms:

No treatment has been proven to reverse vision loss from NAION, according to StatPearls.

“Patients experiencing sudden, painless vision loss in one eye should contact their healthcare professional immediately and should not stop their medication without consulting their provider first,” the FDA told Healthline.

In the announcement, the agency told doctors to promptly refer patients with suspected NAION to an ophthalmologist.

The FDA listed these possible risk factors for NAION:

The FDA told Healthline that many people who take GLP-1s also have these conditions, which made reports of NAION in GLP-1 users “challenging” to interpret.

Across 126 clinical trials, the FDA found about 6 NAION cases per 100,000 people taking a GLP-1 for a year, and about 4 per 100,000 people taking a placebo or another diabetes drug for a year.In real-world data, NAION was more common with GLP-1s than with one type of diabetes pill, but the FDA found no significant increase compared with another type, the agency told Healthline.Zilbermint said patients who saw the news have worried about losing their vision. He tells them that “many of the serious harms, including the potential harm of NAION, are rare.”“The overall risk is still very small,” he said.

Regulators in other countries have added NAION warnings, mostly for semaglutide:

The FDA told Healthline the benefits of GLP-1s “continue to outweigh their risks when used as directed.”The FDA has also approved some GLP-1s to lower the risk of heart attack and stroke, reduce the risk of worsening kidney disease, and treat sleep apnea and a type of liver disease.Zilbermint said that “for most of my patients with obesity, diabetes and heart disease, the benefits clearly outweigh the risks.”“But we definitely have to decide patient by patient,” he added.

The labeling change applies to these medications, according to the FDA’s announcement:

As of publication, the FDA’s online label database did not yet show the new warning on the drugs’ labels.

If ever there was a story detailing the importance of attitude over ability, then this tale of overcoming adversity – in the end –  involving one of West Ham’s worst transfers of the Steidten era – is certainly that.

Having vowed never to write another Niclas Fullkrug story I find myself drawn yet again to his remarkable mental resilience and fortitude, re-launching his career at 34 years old ‘back home’ in Germany after failing so conclusively at both West Ham and AC Milan – just in case anyone wants to point the finger at a Hammers’ coaching failure being solely to blame for his misfiring.

This is a player who lost his international squad spot, couldn’t stay fit for love nor money (although he had plenty of that) and eventually after a very public, failed loan in Italy, rediscovered himself and his form back in his Bundesliga home.

Life in the old dog yet at 34 – Niclas Fullkrug

Fullkrug is now the German top league’s top scorer. Just reflect on that for a second.

The guy who scored three times in two years as a West Ham player. Probably he’d have done better without missing half the club’s games through injury. As Bild.de suggest, Fullkrug has found his happy place and may never leave:

“It is possible!:Will Füllkrug stay with Werder forever?”

Runs their headline, confirming that they want to keep ‘Fulle’ even once his playing days are over:

“There have already been talks about a follow-up contract with us and the idea is still concrete. It is quite conceivable that we will tie him to the club after his career. I really appreciate his football expertise.”

Which suggests that Fullkrug has already done enough to earn another deal at Werder Bremen, meaning he’s likely never, ever coming back. Sadly, neither is the £27 million, or the nine million salary which he’s had as a West Ham player – thats three million per goal. Nice work if you can get it.

From the old Bobby Moore Upper to the Billy Bonds' stand these days - I've been watching since '03 and a supporter since about 1970.. Favourite player - Dean Ashton: Curse you Shaun Wright-Phillips. I still watch YouTube repeats of the Cup Final of 2006 hoping in vain that Shaka Hislop grows another six inches and stops Steven Gerrard's injury time equaliser. Can tell I'm getting old knowing I watched both Mark Noble's debut and his last game at West Ham. Pulling on a Claret and Blue replica shirt still makes me feel the same butterflies as when I was seven years old. Magic.

We have to demand a fee for him at the end of his fantastic goal scoring year (more goals than Kane currently) or tell him to come back. Someone will cough up say 3m for him.

Apple's new iPad mini is perfect in every way (except one)

📅 2026-11-10  |  Read Source →

For fans of the tiny tablet, this is a worthy upgrade.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

For what feels like the first time in years, Apple is in experimenting mode. From folding iPhones to touchscreen MacBooks, the company is suddenly dropping innovative products rather than incremental upgrades. But while the new iPad mini might not be the most exciting new release of 2026, for a select group of users, it might just be the best iPad yet.

The 8th generation iPad mini upgrades almost everything about the tiny tablet. The headline feature has to be the OLED display, which is brighter, crisper and more vibrant than any iPad mini display we've seen. Meanwhile, minor design changes have resulted in a thinner and lighter body – which is a much more meaningful change than it might sound.

I've always been a fan of the iPad mini. Sure, larger models like the Pro feature M-series chips and ProMotion displays, but sometimes there's no replacement for portability. As the famous saying goes, 'your best iPad is the one you have with you' – and being able to sling the iPad mini into a tote bag is invaluable. And the first thing I noticed when picking it up was the reduction in size. At 5.4mm thick and 255 grams, it's just ridiculously thin and light.

And the second thing I noticed was, yes, that OLED display. While it isn't quite as stunning as the new MacBook Pro's PROLED XDR display, those bright colours and inky blacks look stunning. For creatives, a bright, colour-accurate screen is a must, and now the smallest iPad delivers one in spades.

But there is a glaring omission. In this day and age, a 60hz refresh rate feels slow and stuttering on any device, let alone one that starts at £699. Apple's 120hz ProMotion display has made its way to most of its products by now, but it's surprising, given the scale of the rest of the updates (and the price increase) that the iPad mini hasn't been blessed with one. Reddit, as you might imagine, has not been kind.

But if you can put aside the lack of ProMotion, there's so much to love here. Performance-wise, that A20 Pro chip (which also resides in the iPhone 18 Pro and iPhone Duo) is incredibly speedy and efficient – I'm managing to eke out around ten hours on a single charge.

Another improvement is the sound quality. Apple has somehow managed to place the speakers underneath the display (or as Apple calls it, the 'acoustic display'), and the result is powerfully punchy audio for a tablet so small.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

Overall, if you're somebody who already loves the iPad mini and has the cash to burn for an upgrade, the 8th generation model is a winner. Every improvement adds up to a meaningfully enhanced experience, particularly the OLED screen and the weight loss. Now, if Apple could just bring itself to give us a 120hz display, the 9th generation iPad mini might just be the perfect tiny tablet.
